Approximation of Dirac operators with 𝜹 \boldsymbol{\delta} -shell potentials in the norm resolvent sense
Jussi Behrndt, Markus Holzmann, Christian Stelzer

Abstract
. In this paper the approximation of Dirac operators with general δ \delta -shell potentials supported on C 2 C^{2} -curves in ℝ 2 \mathbb{R}^{2} or C 2 C^{2} -surfaces in ℝ 3 \mathbb{R}^{3} , which may be bounded or unbounded, is studied. It is shown under suitable conditions on the weight of the δ \delta -interaction that a family of Dirac operators with regular, squeezed potentials converges in the norm resolvent sense to the Dirac operator with the δ \delta -shell interaction.



1.
Introduction

In mathematical physics, singular potentials supported on a set
\Sigma
of measure zero are often used as replacements of potentials that have large values in a vicinity of
\Sigma
and small values elsewhere, assuming that such idealized models have similar (spectral) properties as the original ones. Nonrelativistic Schrödinger operators with
\delta
-potentials supported on points were already considered in the early days of quantum mechanics
[
28
,
42
]
, see also the monograph
[
1
]
, and then later also Schrödinger operators with
\delta
-potentials supported on curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and similar structures in higher dimensions were investigated, see, e.g.,
[
12
,
15
,
21
]
and the references therein. Concerning relativistic Dirac operators with a singular
\delta
-potential, the one-dimensional case was first studied in the 1980s
[
1
,
22
]
, and recently also two and three-dimensional Dirac operators coupled with
\delta
-shell potentials supported on general curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
were treated; cf.
[
2
,
3
,
9
,
10
,
16
]
and the discussion below.

In all of the above situations, it is necessary to justify that the differential operators with the singular interactions have indeed similar properties as the original models with regular potentials. One way to do this is to prove suitable approximation results: One constructs a family of potentials that converge to the idealized singular interaction and shows that the associated differential operators also converge in the norm resolvent sense to the idealized operator – then also the spectral properties of the approximating and the idealized models are approximately the same
[
27
,
37
]
. For Schrödinger operators with singular potentials this approximation problem has been solved under the assumption that the interaction support is a sufficiently smooth hypersurface in
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\geq 1
; cf.
[
4
]
and the references therein.

The literature on the approximation of Dirac operators with singular potentials is less complete than for their nonrelativistic counterparts. The Dirac operator with a singular
\delta
-potential in
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{1,2,3\}
, is formally given by

-i\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\alpha_{j}\partial_{j}+m\beta+\widetilde{V}\delta_{\Sigma},

(1.1)

where
\delta_{\Sigma}
denotes the
\delta
-distribution supported on a point in
\mathbb{R}
or the boundary
\Sigma
of a bounded or unbounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
or
\mathbb{R}^{3}
,

\widetilde{V}
is a matrix-valued function on
\Sigma
modeling the position-dependent strength of the singular interaction,
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
and
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta},
and
\beta
denote the Dirac matrices defined in (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) below (in dimension
\theta=1
one can use the same choice as for
\theta=2
).
The first approximation result in dimension one goes back to Šeba
[
38
]
, where the norm resolvent convergence for so-called electrostatic (i.e.
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\eta}I_{2}
) and Lorentz scalar (i.e.
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{\tau}\beta
) interactions supported on a point was proved.
In
[
38
]
it already turned out that one has to renormalize the interaction strength in order to obtain convergence, i.e. when
v_{\varepsilon}\rightarrow\eta\delta_{\Sigma}
in the distributional sense, then
-i\alpha_{1}\partial_{x}+m\beta+v_{\varepsilon}I_{2}
does not converge to
-i\alpha_{1}\partial_{x}+m\beta+\eta I_{2}\delta_{\Sigma}
, but to a similar operator, where the coefficient of
\delta_{\Sigma}
depends in a nonlinear way on
\eta
; for Lorentz scalar potentials a similar effect appears as well. Šeba suggested that this renormalization is related to Klein’s paradox for the Dirac equation.
Later Hughes proved in
[
24
,
25
]
strong resolvent convergence for general point interactions and also determined the exact form of the renormalization
in the one-dimensional situation. Recently, Tušek proved norm resolvent convergence in the one-dimensional case for Dirac operators with general point interactions in
[
43
]
.
In the two and three-dimensional setting the approximation problem is not as well-studied as in dimension one and so far
there exist only results on strong resolvent convergence. In dimension three the approximation of Dirac operators with purely electrostatic or Lorentz scalar
\delta
-shell potentials that fulfil a certain smallness condition supported on boundaries of bounded domains were investigated in
[
29
,
30
]
. The approximation of two-dimensional Dirac operators with electrostatic, Lorentz scalar, and anomalous magnetic
\delta
-shell potentials supported on closed bounded curves and on a straight line was considered in
[
16
]
and
[
11
]
, respectively. We note that also in the two and three-dimensional setting a renormalization of the interaction strength was observed in
[
11
,
16
,
30
]
.

In this paper we study the approximation problem for two- and three-dimensional Dirac operators with
\delta
-shell potentials and improve the present
state of art in the following three ways: Under suitable conditions we show (i) norm resolvent convergence on (ii) bounded and unbounded supports
\Sigma
with (iii) general position-dependent interaction strengths
\widetilde{V}
. More precisely:

(i)

Instead of strong resolvent convergence we prove the norm resolvent convergence of the approximating family, which has not been
established in the multi-dimensional situation so far. This type of convergence ensures that the spectrum of the limit operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
can be completely characterized by the spectra of the approximating operators and it also implies the convergence of the related spectral projections.

(ii)

Instead of bounded curves in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, bounded surfaces in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, or the straight line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we treat a general class
of bounded and unbounded interaction supports
\Sigma
which can be described by finitely many rotated graphs of
C^{2}
-functions with bounded
derivatives
(see Hypothesis
2.1
) and which includes, in particular, graphs of
C^{2}
-functions with bounded derivatives and boundaries of bounded
C^{2}
-domains.

(iii)

Instead of considering only electrostatic, Lorentz scalar, and anomalous magnetic interactions (which can be described by three real-valued functions) we allow general symmetric
2\times 2
or
4\times 4
matrix-valued functions as interaction strengths in dimensions two or three, respectively, and provide an explicit formula for the nonlinear renormalization when passing to the limit.

In the following we explain the approximation procedure and our main result in more detail. For this some notation needs to be fixed.
We denote the space dimension by
\theta\in\{2,3\}
and set
N=2
for
\theta=2
and
N=4
for
\theta=3
. Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
be the boundary of a bounded or unbounded
C^{2}
-domain
\Omega_{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
that satisfies
Hypothesis
2.1
. The curve or surface
\Sigma
splits
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
into two disjoint parts
\Omega_{+}
and
\Omega_{-}:=\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\overline{\Omega_{+}}
, and
\nu
is the unit normal vector field at
\Sigma
pointing outwards of
\Omega_{+}
.
For
u:\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{N}
we write
u_{\pm}:=u\upharpoonright\Omega_{\pm}
and we denote the Dirichlet trace operator by
\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}:H^{1}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, where
H^{s}
are the
L^{2}
-based Sobolev spaces. Moreover,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta},\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are the Dirac matrices defined in (
1.15
)–(
1.16
) and we make use of the notations

\alpha\cdot\nabla:=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\alpha_{j}\partial_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\cdot x:=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\alpha_{j}x_{j},\quad x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{C}^{\theta}.

We introduce for
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\widetilde{V}\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
such that
\widetilde{V}(x_{\Sigma})=(\widetilde{V}(x_{\Sigma}))^{*}
for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
the operator

\begin{split}H_{\widetilde{V}}u&:=\big(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta\big)u_{+}\oplus\big(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta\big)u_{-},\\
\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}&:=\bigg\{u\in H^{1}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{1}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N}):\\
&\quad\qquad i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-})+\frac{\widetilde{V}}{2}(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-})=0\bigg\}.\end{split}

(1.2)

This is the rigorous definition of the formal operator (
1.1
), which was studied under various assumptions on the coefficients
\widetilde{V}
and the interaction support
\Sigma
in
[
2
,
3
,
5
,
6
,
9
,
10
,
13
,
16
,
35
,
36
]
. However,
under the general assumptions in this paper even the self-adjointness of
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is not yet established, but
will follow as a byproduct of our main result below as
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is the norm resolvent limit of a sequence of self-adjoint operators.

It is the main goal of the present paper to show that the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) can be approximated in the norm resolvent sense by Dirac operators with strongly localized potentials. To introduce the latter operators, define the map

\begin{split}\iota:\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\theta},\qquad\iota(x_{\Sigma},t):=x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma}),\end{split}

(1.3)

and for
\varepsilon>0
the set

\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\iota(\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)),\quad\varepsilon>0.

(1.4)

We call
\Omega_{\varepsilon}

tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma
. Hypothesis
2.1
and Proposition
2.4
imply that there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that
\iota|_{\mathbb{R}\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})}
is injective and, hence, all points in
\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}
can be uniquely described by the map
\iota
.
To define the above mentioned strongly localized potentials choose

q\in L^{\infty}((-1,1);\mathbb{R})\quad\text{with}\quad\int_{-1}^{1}q(s)\,ds=1

(1.5)

and

V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})\quad\text{such that}\quad V(x_{\Sigma})=(V(x_{\Sigma}))^{*}\text{ for }\sigma\text{-a.e. }x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma,

(1.6)

where
W^{1}_{\infty}
denotes the first order
L^{\infty}
-based Sobolev space.
Since
\iota|_{\mathbb{R}\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})}
is injective, we can define for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})

V_{\varepsilon}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}V(x_{\Sigma})q\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right),&\text{if }x=\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)\in\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
0,&\text{if }x\notin\Omega_{\varepsilon},\end{cases}

(1.7)

and for
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
the operator

\begin{split}H_{\varepsilon}u&:=-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)u+m\beta u+V_{\varepsilon}u,\quad\textup{dom }H_{\varepsilon}:=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}).\end{split}

(1.8)

Note that
H_{\varepsilon}
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, as
V_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
is symmetric and the unperturbed Dirac operator
H=H_{\varepsilon}-V_{\varepsilon}
defined on
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
is self-adjoint in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
; cf. Section
2.3
.

The sequence
V_{\varepsilon}
converges to
V\delta_{\Sigma}
in the sense of distributions. However, as in
[
11
,
16
,
24
,
25
,
30
,
38
,
43
]
the sequence
H_{\varepsilon}
does not converge to
H_{V}
, but to a similar operator with a renormalized interaction strength
\widetilde{V}
. In the present situation this renormalization is expressed with the help of the matrix-valued function

S=\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1},

(1.9)

where analytic functions of matrices are defined via the corresponding power series, or equivalently via the Riesz-Dunford functional calculus.
Now we are prepared to formulate the main result of this paper.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, and assume that for some
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
the condition

{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}<X_{z}

(1.10)

holds with
X_{z}
given by (
3.28
) and that
\varepsilon_{2}>0
satisfies (
3.11
). Furthermore, assume that

\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})

(1.11)

and set
\widetilde{V}=VS
, where
S
is defined as in (
1.9
). Then the operator

H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,

z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})\cap\rho(H_{\widetilde{V}})
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
, and
for any
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
there exists
C>0
such that

{\left\lVert{(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}).

In particular,
H_{\varepsilon}
converges to
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in the norm resolvent sense as
\varepsilon\to 0+
.

The main assumption in Theorem
1.1
is condition (
1.10
), which means that
V
has to be small in a suitable sense and which also appears in a similar form in
[
30
]
, where the strong resolvent convergence of
H_{\varepsilon}
for some special potentials
V
was proved. The condition for
V
can be optimized by choosing
q=\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{(-1,1)}
, where
\chi_{(-1,1)}
denotes the characteristic function of the interval
(-1,1)
, but since for some applications also more general
q
can be useful, we keep the more general formulation of (
1.10
); a more detailed discussion of (
1.10
)
can be found in Remark
4.5
. We shall show later in Lemma
3.13
that

X_{z}\leq\frac{\pi}{4}
, if
\Sigma
is compact and smooth.

In the following, let us consider the special choice

V=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta+\lambda i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\beta,

(1.12)

where
\eta,\tau,\lambda\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
are real functions (or simply real constants) that model electrostatic, Lorentz scalar, and anomalous magnetic interactions, respectively. Such potentials
V
are of special physical interest and the existing literature on Dirac operators with
\delta
-shell potentials mostly focuses on this case.
It turns out that for such
V

the renormalization matrix in (
1.9
) has the simple form

S=\tfrac{2\tan(\sqrt{d}/2)}{\sqrt{d}}I_{N},

where
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}-\lambda^{2}
, and (
1.11
) simplifies, see (
1.13
) below. Note that analogous conditions and renormalizations are given in
[
16
, Theorem 2.6]
,
[
29
, Theorem IV.2]
and
[
38
, Remarks]
.
In the present situation Theorem
1.1
reduces to the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.12
), respectively, with
\eta,\tau,\lambda\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
,
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}-\lambda^{2}
, and assume that for some
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
condition (
1.10
)
holds with
X_{z}
given by (
3.28
) and that
\varepsilon_{2}>0
satisfies (
3.11
). Furthermore, assume

\inf_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left\lvert{(2k+1)^{2}\pi^{2}-d(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert>0

(1.13)

and let
\widetilde{V}=\frac{2\tan(\sqrt{d}/2)}{\sqrt{d}}V
. Then,

H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,

z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})\cap\rho(H_{\widetilde{V}})
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
, and
for any
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
there exists
C>0
such that

{\left\lVert{(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}).

In particular,
H_{\varepsilon}
converges to
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in the norm resolvent sense as
\varepsilon\to 0+
.

In the previous two results we stated conditions on a given
V
ensuring that
H_{\varepsilon}
converges in the norm resolvent sense to
H_{\widetilde{V}}
. In the following corollary, we address the inverse question and state conditions on
\widetilde{V}
such that
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is the limit of the operators
H_{\varepsilon}
.

Corollary 1.3
.

Let
\widetilde{V}\in W_{\infty}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
such that
\widetilde{V}(x_{\Sigma})=(\widetilde{V}(x_{\Sigma}))^{*}
for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, and assume that for some given
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))

\|(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}\|_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}<2\tanh\big(\tfrac{X_{z}}{{\left\lVert{\alpha\cdot\nu}\right\rVert}_{W_{\infty}^{1}(\Sigma;C^{N\times N})}}\big)

(1.14)

holds and that
\varepsilon_{2}>0
satisfies (
3.11
).
Moreover, set
q=\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{(-1,1)}
and

V=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V})^{2n+1}}{2^{2n+1}(2n+1)},

and let
H_{\varepsilon}
be the operator defined in (
1.8
) with these specific choices of
q
and
V
.
Then, the operator

H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,

z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})\cap\rho(H_{\widetilde{V}})
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
, and
for any
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
there exists
C>0
such that

{\left\lVert{(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\qquad\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}).

In particular,
H_{\varepsilon}
converges to
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in the norm resolvent sense as
\varepsilon\to 0+
.

Let us briefly explain the strategy to prove Theorem
1.1
and the structure of this paper.
First, in Section
2
we collect some preliminary results regarding the geometry of
\Sigma
, the free Dirac operator, and some associated potential and boundary integral operators (see also Appendix
C
).
In
Proposition
3.2
we then prove the resolvent formula

(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}=(H-z)^{-1}-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z),

where
H
is the free Dirac operator (see (
2.8
)) and
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z),C_{\varepsilon}(z)
are suitable integral operators defined in (
3.4
) that are bounded between various
L^{2}
-spaces; cf. Proposition
3.1
.
This is a standard approach that has been applied in other
approximation problems for differential operators with singular potentials, see, e.g.,
[
1
,
4
,
30
,
38
]
.
Typically, the convergence of
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z),C_{\varepsilon}(z)
is analyzed in the corresponding
L^{2}
-setting. In the situation of Dirac operators with singular potentials,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
converges only strongly in
L^{2}
and hence, via this approach only the strong resolvent convergence of
H_{\varepsilon}
can be shown; cf.
[
30
]
. Contrary to that, we investigate the convergence of
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z),C_{\varepsilon}(z)
between Sobolev type spaces. For this, a certain
shift operator, which allows to compare the values of functions in
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and in
x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon s\nu(x_{\Sigma})
,
plays a crucial role; cf. Proposition
3.3
.
The condition (
1.10
) in Theorem
1.1
is needed to ensure that
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
.
Finally, using the limiting behavior of
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z),C_{\varepsilon}(z)
we compute in Section
4
the limit of
(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}
for
\varepsilon\to 0+
and show that it indeed coincides with the resolvent of
H_{\widetilde{V}}
.
In Appendices
A
and
B
we provide some technical calculations that are necessary for the definition of the tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma
and the convergence analysis.

Notations

By
\theta\in\{2,3\}
we denote the space dimension and we set
N=2
for
\theta=2
and
N=4
for
\theta=3
. Recall that the Pauli spin matrices are

\sigma_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\sigma_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-i\\
i&0\end{pmatrix},\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

With their help the Dirac matrices
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta},\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are defined for
\theta=2
by

\alpha_{1}:=\sigma_{1},\quad\alpha_{2}:=\sigma_{2},\quad\text{and}\quad\beta:=\sigma_{3},

(1.15)

and for
\theta=3
by

\alpha_{j}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma_{j}\\
\sigma_{j}&0\end{pmatrix}\text{ for }j=1,2,3\quad\text{and}\quad\beta:=\begin{pmatrix}I_{2}&0\\
0&-I_{2}\end{pmatrix},

(1.16)

where
I_{2}
is the
2\times 2
-identity matrix.
We will often make use of the notations

\alpha\cdot\nabla:=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\alpha_{j}\partial_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\cdot x:=\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\alpha_{j}x_{j},\quad x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{C}^{\theta}.

The letter
C>0
always denotes a constant which may change in-between lines and does not depend on the space variables or on
\varepsilon
.
The symbol
|\cdot|
is used for the absolute value, the Euclidean vector norm, or the Frobenius norm of a number, a vector, or a matrix, respectively.
We write
{\left\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\rangle}
for the Euclidean scalar product in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which is anti-linear in the second argument. Eventually, the application of an analytic function to a matrix
A
is defined via the associated power series, whenever it converges. This implies, in particular, for two holomorphic functions
f,g
that
f(A)g(A)=(fg)(A)
. We will also make use of the equivalent definition of
f(A)
via the Riesz-Dunford calculus

f(A)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial\Omega}f(z)(A-z)^{-1}dz,

(1.17)

where
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
such that all eigenvalues of
A
belong to
\Omega
,
f
is holomorphic in a neighborhood of
\Omega
, and the integral is understood as a complex line integral; cf.
[
19
, Chapter VII. §4]
and
[
20
, Chapter VIII.3.1]
.

Next, let
\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}
be Hilbert spaces and let
A
be a linear operator from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{G}
. The domain, kernel, and range of
A
are denoted by
\textup{dom }A
,
\ker A
, and
\textup{ran }A
, respectively. If
A
is bounded and everywhere defined, then we write
\|A\|_{\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}}
for its operator norm. If
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{G}
and
A
is closed, then the resolvent set and the spectrum
A
are denoted by
\rho(A)
and
\sigma(A)
, respectively.

Following
[
32
, Appendix B]
, we call
(\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1})
a compatible pair, if
\mathcal{H}_{0}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
are two Hilbert spaces which are continuously embedded in a bigger Hausdorff topological vector space. In this situation, one can construct with the K-method (or various other methods, see
[
17
,
18
,
26
,
32
]
, which yield the same spaces with equivalent norms) a family of Hilbert spaces

[\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}]_{\tau}
,
\tau\in(0,1)
, such that
\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}\subset[\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}]_{\tau}\subset\mathcal{H}_{0}+\mathcal{H}_{1}
for all
\tau\in(0,1)
. Assume that
(\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{1})
is another compatible pair of Hilbert spaces. Recall that for two bounded operators
A_{0}:\mathcal{H}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}
and
A_{1}:\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}
such that

A_{0}u=A_{1}u
for all
u\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
, there exists by
[
32
, Theorem B.2]
a unique bounded linear operator
A_{\tau}:[\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}]_{\tau}\to[\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{1}]_{\tau}
such that
A_{0}u=A_{1}u=A_{\tau}u
for all
u\in\mathcal{H}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}_{1}
and

{\left\lVert{A_{\tau}}\right\rVert}_{[\mathcal{H}_{0},\mathcal{H}_{1}]_{\tau}\to[\mathcal{G}_{0},\mathcal{G}_{1}]_{\tau}}\leq{\left\lVert{A_{0}}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}_{0}\to\mathcal{G}_{0}}^{1-\tau}{\left\lVert{A_{1}}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}_{1}\to\mathcal{G}_{1}}^{\tau}.

(1.18)

Finally, if
k,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open set and
\mathcal{V}
denotes the space of real or complex scalars, vectors, or matrices, then we write
C_{b}^{k}(U;\mathcal{V})
for the space of all
f\in C^{k}(U;\mathcal{V})
such that
f
and all partial derivatives of
f
up to order
k
are bounded. If
f\in C^{1}(U;\mathbb{R}^{k})
, then the symbol
Df
is used for the Jacobi matrix of
f
.
The usual Bochner Lebesgue space of
\mathcal{H}
-valued
functions are denoted by
L^{2}((-1,1);\mathcal{H})
, cf. Section
2.2
. For
\mathcal{H}=H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
we use the symbol

{\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{r}
for the norm in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. In a similar way, we write

\begin{split}{\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{r_{1}\to r_{2}}&:={\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r_{1}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r_{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))},\\
{\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{r\to\mathcal{H}}&:={\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to\mathcal{H}},\\
{\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}\to r^{\prime}}&:={\left\lVert{\cdot}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r^{\prime}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}.\end{split}

Acknowledgement.

The authors gratefully acknowledge financial support by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P33568-N. This publication is based upon work from COST Action CA 18232 MAT-DYN-NET, supported by COST (European Cooperation in Science and Technology), www.cost.eu.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section we provide some preliminary material that is needed in the main part of this paper to prove Theorem
1.1
. First, in Subsection
2.1
we formulate suitable assumptions on the hypersurfaces
\Sigma
that are convenient for our approximation procedure, then in Subsection
2.2
we provide some definitions and fundamental results for Bochner Lebesgue spaces that are used throughout this paper, and in Subsection
2.3
we introduce the free Dirac operator and a family of associated potential and boundary integral operators.

2.1.
Description of the boundary
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and its tubular neighbourhood

In this section we formulate assumptions on the geometry of
\Sigma
which will allow us to construct the tubular neighbourhood of
\Sigma
in (
1.4
) in a similar manner as in
[
4
,
30
]
. The presentation here differs slightly from
[
4
]
, as in the present paper we need better properties of the trace map and the extension operator. The following assumption will be made throughout the paper:

Hypothesis 2.1
.

Assume that
\Omega_{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, is an open set with boundary
\Sigma:=\partial\Omega_{+}
and that there exist open sets
W_{1},\dots,W_{p}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
, mappings
\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{p}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
, rotation matrices
\kappa_{1},\dots,\kappa_{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta}
, and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

(i)

\Sigma\subset\bigcup_{l=1}^{p}W_{l}
;

(ii)

if
x\in\partial\Omega_{+}=\Sigma
, then there exists
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that
B(x,\varepsilon_{0})\subset W_{l}
;

(iii)

W_{l}\cap\Omega_{+}=W_{l}\cap\Omega_{l}
, where
\Omega_{l}=\{\kappa_{l}\big(x^{\prime},x_{\theta}\big):x_{\theta}<\zeta(x^{\prime}),\,(x^{\prime},x_{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\}
, for
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.

Furthermore, we set
\Sigma_{l}:=\partial\Omega_{l}=\{\kappa_{l}(x^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})):x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\}
,
\Omega_{-}:=\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\overline{\Omega_{+}}
, and denote the unit normal vector field at
\Sigma
that is pointing outwards of
\Omega_{+}
by
\nu
.

We note that
C_{b}^{2}
-hypographs and the boundaries of compact
C^{2}
-domains satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
.
Moreover, the sets
\Sigma_{l}
are a cover of
\Sigma
, but in general one has
\Sigma_{l}\not\subset\Sigma
.
In the following we recall the construction of Sobolev spaces on
\Sigma
satisfying Hypothesis
2.1
and related results which will be useful in this paper; cf.
[
31
,
32
]
for similar constructions.
First, if
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
is open, then for
r\in[0,2]
the space
H^{r}(U;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and also
W^{1}_{\infty}(U;\mathcal{V})
with
\mathcal{V}\in\{\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}\}
are defined as in
[
32
]
. To transfer these definitions to
\Sigma
,
we choose a partition of unity
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{p}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta})
subordinate to
W_{1},\dots,W_{p}
such that each
\varphi_{l}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, has uniformly bounded derivatives (see Lemma
B.2
). Next, we define

x_{\Sigma_{l}}:\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{l},\qquad x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}):=\kappa_{l}(x^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})),

(2.1)

and for
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
we write

\psi_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}):=\begin{cases}\varphi_{l}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))\psi(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})),&\text{if }x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})\in\Sigma,\\
0,&\text{if }x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})\notin\Sigma.\end{cases}

Then,
\psi_{\Sigma_{l}}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\psi(x_{\Sigma})=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\psi_{\Sigma_{l}}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(x_{\Sigma}))
for
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
. As usual, we introduce for
r\in[0,2]

H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}):=\big\{\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}):\psi_{\Sigma_{l}}\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})\text{ for all }l=1,\dots,p\big\}

and endow this space with the inner product

{\left({\phi},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}{\left({\phi_{\Sigma_{l}}},{\psi_{\Sigma_{l}}}\right)}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})},\qquad\phi,\psi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Sobolev spaces
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
r\in[-2,0]
are defined, as usual, by duality. One can prove in the same manner as in
[
32
, Theorem B.11]
that for
r_{1},r_{2}\in[-2,2]
and
r=(1-\tau)r_{1}+\tau r_{2}
,
0<\tau<1
, the interpolation property

H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})=\left[H^{r_{1}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{r_{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\right]_{\tau}

(2.2)

holds with equivalent norms. Eventually, we set
U_{l}:=x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma\cap W_{l})
and define for
\mathcal{V}\in\{\mathbb{C};\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}\}

W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V}):=\big\{F\in L^{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V}):(F\circ x_{\Sigma_{l}})\upharpoonright U_{l}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(U_{l};\mathcal{V})\text{ for all }l=1,\dots,p\big\}

equipped with the norm

{\left\lVert{F}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V})}:=\max_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}{\left\lVert{(F\circ x_{\Sigma_{l}})\upharpoonright U_{l}}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(U_{l};\mathcal{V})},\quad F\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V}).

Note that if
F\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V})
and
r\in[0,1]
, then by direct calculation (
r\in\{0,1\}
) and interpolation (
r\in(0,1)
) one obtains for all
\phi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})

F\phi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\quad\textup{and}\quad{\left\lVert{F\phi}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C{\left\lVert{F}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V})}{\left\lVert{\phi}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})},

(2.3)

where
C>0
does not depend on
F
. In particular,
\phi\mapsto F\phi
induces a bounded operator in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the operator norm is bounded by
C{\left\lVert{F}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathcal{V})}
. We identify
F
with this induced operator.

In the next proposition, we state a variant of the trace theorem suitable for our situation; cf.
[
31
, Theorem 2]
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Assume that
\Omega_{\pm}
and
\Sigma
satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
and let
r\in\big(\tfrac{1}{2},1\big]
. Then, the following is true:

(i)

There exists a unique bounded and surjective operator

\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}:H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{r-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})

such that
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}u=u|_{\Sigma}
for all
u\in H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap C(\overline{\Omega_{\pm}};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

(ii)

There exists a unique bounded and surjective operator

\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}:H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{r-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})

such that
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}u=u|_{\Sigma}
for all
u\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

In the rest of this subsection we summarize some statements on tubular neighbourhoods of
\Sigma
given by (
1.4
) by making use of the results in
[
4
, Section 2.1]
. For this, some notation is necessary. Recall that the embedding
\iota
is defined by (
1.3
) and that the set
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon>0
is introduced in (
1.4
). In the following definition we introduce the Weingarten map (or shape operator) on
\Sigma
:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\Omega_{\pm},\Sigma

satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
and denote for
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})\in\Sigma
the tangent hyperplane of
\Sigma
in
x_{\Sigma}
by
T_{x_{\Sigma}}:=\textup{span}\,\{\partial_{j}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}):j=1,\dots,\theta-1\}
. The Weingarten map is the linear operator
W(x_{\Sigma}):T_{x_{\Sigma}}\to T_{x_{\Sigma}}
defined by

W(x_{\Sigma})\partial_{j}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=-\partial_{j}\nu(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})),\qquad j\in\{1,\dots,\theta-1\}.

As mentioned in
[
4
, Definition 2.2]
and the text below,
W(x_{\Sigma})
is well-defined (i.e. it is independent of the parametrization of
\Sigma
and the index
l
). Furthermore, we denote the matrix representation of
W(x_{\Sigma})
corresponding to the basis
\{\partial_{j}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}):j=1,\dots,\theta-1\}
of
T_{x_{\Sigma}}
by
L_{l}(x^{\prime})
.
Note that if
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=x_{\Sigma_{k}}(y^{\prime})\in\Sigma
with
l,k\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
, then the eigenvalues of
L_{l}(x^{\prime})
and
L_{k}(y^{\prime})
coincide, see
[
4
, Definition 2.2]
and the text below. Therefore, the expression
\det(I-tW(x_{\Sigma})):=\det(I_{\theta-1}-tL_{l}(x^{\prime}))
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
is well-defined.
In the next proposition we state important properties of
\iota
and
W
,
and identify
L^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
with
L^{1}(\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\Omega_{\pm},\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
. Then, there exists
\varepsilon_{1}>0
such that the following is true:

(i)

\iota|_{\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})}
is injective.

(ii)

There exists
C>0
such that
\left\lvert{1-\det(I-\varepsilon W(x_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon<1/2
for all
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
\varepsilon\in(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
.

(iii)

For
\varepsilon\in(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
one has
u\in L^{1}(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
if and only if
u\circ\iota|_{\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)}\in L^{1}(\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon))
and in this case

\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}u(y)\,dy=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\int_{\Sigma}u(y_{\Sigma}+s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\det(I-sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds.

Proof.

Let
\varepsilon_{A}
be the number specified in Proposition
A.2
and let
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,\varepsilon_{A})
. Then by Proposition
A.2
(ii) there exists
C_{A,2}>0
such that

\left\lvert{\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)-\iota(y_{\Sigma},s)}\right\rvert\geq C_{A,2}^{-1}(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert),\quad(x_{\Sigma},t),(y_{\Sigma},s)\in\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}).

Hence,
\iota|_{\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})}
is injective, i.e. item (i) is true.

Next, we show assertion (ii). For this, we first compute for
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
such that
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})\in\Sigma
an explicit representation of the matrix
L_{l}(x^{\prime})
, i.e. the matrix representation of
W(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))
with respect to the basis
\{\partial_{j}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}):j=1,\dots,\theta-1\}
. Let
e_{1},\dots,e_{\theta}
be the standard basis vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
and note that

\nu(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))=\frac{\kappa_{l}(-\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),1)}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}=-\kappa_{l}\sum_{k=1}^{\theta-1}\frac{\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{k}+\kappa_{l}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{\theta}.

We compute

\begin{split}-\partial_{j}\Bigg(&-\kappa_{l}\sum_{k=1}^{\theta-1}\frac{\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{k}\Bigg)\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{\theta-1}\frac{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}\kappa_{l}e_{k}\end{split}

and

\begin{split}-\partial_{j}\Bigg(&\kappa_{l}\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}e_{\theta}\Bigg)=\kappa_{l}\frac{\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}e_{\theta}\\
&=\frac{\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})-\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}\kappa_{l}e_{\theta}\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{\theta-1}\frac{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}\kappa_{l}\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})e_{\theta}.\end{split}

By combining the latter two equations with
\partial_{k}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(e_{k}+\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})e_{\theta})
, we conclude

\begin{split}W(&x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))\partial_{j}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=-\partial_{j}\nu(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))\\
&=\sum_{k=1}^{\theta-1}\frac{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}\partial_{k}x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),\end{split}

which shows that

L_{l}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\Big((1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})\partial_{j}\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\big\langle\nabla\partial_{j}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\big\rangle\partial_{k}\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\Big)_{k,j\in\{1,\dots,\theta-1\}}}{(1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2})^{3/2}}.

(2.4)

Let
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})\in\Sigma\cap\Sigma_{l}
and recall that
\det(I-\varepsilon W(x_{\Sigma}))=\det(I_{\theta-1}-\varepsilon L_{l}(x^{\prime}))
.
Expressing the determinant as the product of the eigenvalues one verifies

1-\det(I_{\theta-1}-\varepsilon L_{l}(x^{\prime}))=\varepsilon P_{l}(\varepsilon)
, where
P_{l}
is a polynomial in
\varepsilon
with coefficients depending continuously on the entries of
L_{l}(x^{\prime})
. Since
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
by Hypothesis
2.1
, equation (
2.4
) implies
\sup_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\},x^{\prime}\in x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert{L_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert<\infty.
This shows that (ii) holds if
\varepsilon_{1}
is chosen sufficiently small.

Finally, the claim in (iii) follows from
[
4
, Proposition 2.6]
, since (i), (ii), and Proposition
A.2
(i) show that
\Sigma
satisfying Hypothesis
2.1
also fulfils
[
4
, Hypothesis 2.3]
.
∎

2.2.
Bochner Lebesgue spaces

In this subsection we summarize some results on Bochner Lebesgue spaces that will be used throughout this paper; for details we refer to
[
26
, Chapter 1]
. We always assume that
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{G}
are separable Hilbert spaces,
\mathcal{O},\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2}
are Borel sets in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is the set of bounded linear operators from
\mathcal{H}
to
\mathcal{G}
.

Definition 2.5
.

We call
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}
(weakly) measurable, if for all
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}
the mapping
\mathcal{O}\ni t\mapsto{\left({f(t)},{\varphi}\right)}_{\mathcal{H}}
is measurable with respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathcal{O}
. Furthermore, we call
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
measurable, if
\mathcal{O}\ni t\mapsto F(t)h
is measurable for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
.

We recall for completeness that a function
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}
is (strongly) measurable, if
f
is the pointwise limit of simple functions,
and that in the present situation both notions of measurability coincide due to Pettis theorem, see
[
26
, Theorem 1.1.20]
. Moreover, if
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}
and
F:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
are measurable, then the function
\mathcal{O}\ni t\mapsto F(t)f(t)\in\mathcal{G}
is measurable, see
[
26
, Proposition 1.1.28]
.

For a measurable function
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}
such that
t\mapsto{\left\lVert{f(t)}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}}
is integrable, the Bochner integral
\int_{\mathcal{O}}f(t)\,dt\in\mathcal{H}
is defined in the standard way, see
[
26
, Definition 1.2.1 and Proposition 1.2.2]
. Many standard results for
(usual) Lebesgue integrals admit natural generalizations to Bochner integrals. In particular, we will make use of Fubini’s theorem for Bochner integrals, see
[
26
, Proposition 1.2.7]
: If
f:\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathcal{O}_{2}\to\mathcal{H}
is measurable and the integral
\int_{\mathcal{O}_{1}\times\mathcal{O}_{2}}{\left\lVert{f(t,s)}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}}\,\,dt\,ds
is finite, then the Bochner integral
\int_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}f(t,s)\,ds
exists for a.e.
t\in\mathcal{O}_{1}
and the function
\mathcal{O}_{1}\ni t\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{O}_{2}}f(t,s)
is measurable and Bochner integrable.

Next, we introduce Bochner Lebesgue spaces.

Definition 2.6
.

We define
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H})
as the space which contains all (equivalence classes of) measurable functions
f:\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{H}
such that

\int_{\mathcal{O}}{\left\lVert{f(t)}\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,dt<\infty.

Furthermore, we equip this space with the scalar product

\int_{\mathcal{O}}{\left({f(t)},{g(t)}\right)}_{\mathcal{H}}\,dt,\qquad f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}).

It is not difficult to show that
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H})
is a Hilbert space; cf. the comments below
[
26
, Definition 1.2.15]
.

Next, duality and interpolation properties of Bochner Lebesgue spaces are discussed. According to
[
26
, Corollary 1.3.13 and Theorem 1.3.21]
, the identification of
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}^{*})
with the functional defined by

L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H})\ni g\mapsto\int_{\mathcal{O}}{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}}{\left\langle{f(t)},{g(t)}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}\,dt,

where
{{}_{\mathcal{H}^{*}}}{\left\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{H}}
denotes the linear dual pairing on
\mathcal{H}^{*}\times\mathcal{H}
,
induces an isometric isomorphism between
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}^{*})
and the dual space of
L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H})
, i.e.

L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H})^{*}\simeq L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}^{*}).

(2.5)

If
\mathcal{G}
is a Hilbert space such that
(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G})
is a compatible pair (cf. the notations section), then also
(L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}),L^{2}(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{G}))
is a compatible pair and

L^{2}\big(\mathcal{O};[\mathcal{H},\mathcal{G}]_{\tau}\big)=\big[L^{2}\big(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{H}\big),L^{2}\big(\mathcal{O};\mathcal{G}\big)\big]_{\tau},\quad\tau\in(0,1),

(2.6)

with equivalent norms, see
[
26
, Theorem 2.2.6 and Corollary C.4.2]
.

In this paper we are particularly interested in the Bochner Lebesgue spaces
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
,
r\in[-2,2]
, where
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, is a hypersurface satisfying Hypothesis
2.1
and
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
is defined as in Section
2.1
. We summarize important properties of these spaces in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\Omega_{\pm},\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
. Then, the following is true:

(i)

If
\tau\in(0,1)
,
r_{1},r_{2}\in[-2,2]
and
r=(1-\tau)r_{1}+\tau r_{2}
, then

\begin{split}&L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\\
&\hskip 30.0pt=\big[L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r_{1}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big),L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r_{2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\big]_{\tau}\end{split}

and the corresponding norms are equivalent.

(ii)

For
r\in[0,2]
there exists an isometric isomorphism between the dual space of
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, i.e.

L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)^{*}\simeq L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{-r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big).

(iii)

The identification of
F\in L^{2}(\Sigma\times(-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
with the function
f:t\mapsto F(\cdot,t)
induces an isometric isomorphism between
L^{2}(\Sigma\times(-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and

\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)\,dt\bigg)(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)(x_{\Sigma})\,dt\quad\textup{ for }\sigma\textup{-a.e. }x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma.

Proof.

Items (i) and (ii) follow from (
2.6
) and (
2.5
) and the properties of
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
in Section
2.1
and (
2.2
). Assertion (iii) follows from
[
26
, Proposition 1.2.24 and Proposition 1.2.25]
.
∎

The following identification turns out to be useful in our considerations: For

\mathcal{Q}\in L^{\infty}((-1,1);\mathbb{C})
and a bounded operator
\mathcal{A}
in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
r\in[-2,2]
, we identify

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}:L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\to L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big),\quad\!\!(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}f)(t):=\mathcal{Q}(t)f(t),

and

\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}:L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\to L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big),\quad(\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}f)(t):=\mathcal{A}(f(t)),

with
\mathcal{Q}
and
\mathcal{A}
, respectively. Note that the norms
{\left\lVert{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\right\rVert}_{r\to r}
and
{\left\lVert{\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{A}}}\right\rVert}_{r\to r}
are bounded by
{\left\lVert{\mathcal{Q}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1);\mathbb{C})}
and
{\left\lVert{\mathcal{A}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
, respectively.
We will also use the bounded embedding

\mathfrak{J}:H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big),\quad(\mathfrak{J}\varphi)(t):=\varphi,

(2.7)

and its adjoint

\mathfrak{J}^{*}:L^{2}\big((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\to H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),\quad\mathfrak{J}^{*}f=\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)\,dt.

2.3.
The free Dirac operator and associated integral operators

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and recall that the Dirac matrices
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\theta},\beta\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
are given by (
1.15
)–(
1.16
). Then the free Dirac operator
H
is the differential operator in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
given by

\begin{split}Hu&:=-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)u+m\beta u,\qquad\textup{dom }H:=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}).\end{split}

(2.8)

With the help of the Fourier transform one gets that
H
is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\sigma(H)=\left(-\infty,-\left\lvert{m}\right\rvert\right]\cup\left[\left\lvert{m}\right\rvert,\infty\right)
, see for instance
[
11
, Section 2]
for
\theta=2
and
[
41
, Theorem 1.1]
for
\theta=3
. For
z\notin\sigma(H)
the resolvent
R_{z}
is

R_{z}u(x):=(H-z)^{-1}u(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x-y)u(y)\,dy,\qquad u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),~x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta},

(2.9)

where
G_{z}
is given for
\theta=2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
by

\begin{split}G_{z}(x)&=\frac{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}}{2\pi}K_{1}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\frac{\alpha\cdot x}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt+\frac{1}{2\pi}K_{0}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\left(m\beta+zI_{2}\right)\end{split}

(2.10)

and for
\theta=3
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
by

G_{z}(x)=\left(zI_{4}+m\beta+i\left(1-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\right)\frac{\alpha\cdot x}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{2}}\right)\frac{e^{i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}}{4\pi\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert};

(2.11)

cf., e.g.,
[
9
,
10
,
41
]
.
Here,
K_{0}
and
K_{1}
denote the modified Bessel functions of the second kind of order zero and one, respectively, and the branch of the square root is fixed by
\text{Im}\,\sqrt{w}>0
for
w\in\mathbb{C}\setminus[0,\infty)
.
Note that
R_{z}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and it can also be viewed as a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. In fact, with the help of the Fourier transform, it is not difficult to show that
R_{z}
gives rise to a bounded operator from
H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

We move on to the discussion of potential and boundary integral operators associated with the free Dirac operator. In the following, let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
be fixed and let
\Omega_{\pm}
and
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
. First, we introduce the potential operator
\Phi_{z}:L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\displaystyle\Phi_{z}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma})\varphi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}),\qquad\varphi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),~x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

(2.12)

We note that
\Phi_{z}
is indeed well-defined and bounded, see
[
2
, Lemma 2.1]
.
Further properties of
\Phi_{z}
are summarized in the following proposition. For compact hypersurfaces
\Sigma
, these results are well known, see, e.g.,
[
10
, Theorem 4.3]
, but for unbounded
\Sigma
they were not treated in the literature so far. For completeness, we give a proof of these results in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
and let
\Phi_{z}
be given by (
2.12
). Then, the following is true:

(i)

For any
r\in\big[0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big]
the operator
\Phi_{z}
gives rise to a bounded operator

\begin{split}\Phi_{z}:H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r+1/2}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{r+1/2}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N}).\end{split}

(ii)

For
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
one has
[(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})\Phi_{z}\varphi]_{\pm}=0
.

(iii)

The adjoint
\Phi_{z}^{*}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
of
\Phi_{z}
acts on
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as

\Phi_{z}^{*}u(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{\overline{z}}(x_{\Sigma}-y)u(y)\,dy=\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}R_{\overline{z}}u(x_{\Sigma}),\qquad x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma,

(2.13)

and
\Phi_{z}^{*}
gives rise to a bounded operator
\Phi_{z}^{*}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Finally, we introduce a family of boundary integral operators for the Dirac equation. Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
. Then, we define the map
\mathcal{C}_{z}:H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}(\Phi_{z}\varphi)_{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}(\Phi_{z}\varphi)_{-}\big),\qquad\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(2.14)

We remark that the operator
\mathcal{C}_{z}
can be represented as a strongly singular boundary integral operator, see for instance
[
10
, eq. (4.5) and Proposition 4.4 (ii)]
for the case that
\Omega_{+}
is bounded. However, for our purposes the representation in (
2.14
) is more convenient.
The basic properties of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
are stated in the following proposition. Again, for compact hypersurfaces
\Sigma
they are well-known, see, e.g.,
[
10
, Theorem 4.3, Proposition 4.4, and Corollary 4.5]
. For general (also unbounded) hypersurfaces
\Sigma
satisfying Hypothesis
2.1
we give a proof in Appendix
C
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
and let
\mathcal{C}_{z}
be given by (
2.14
). Then, the following is true:

(i)

For any
r\in\big[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\big]
the map
\mathcal{C}_{z}
has a bounded extension
\mathcal{C}_{z}:H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

(ii)

For any
r\in\big(0,\frac{1}{2}\big]
and
\varphi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
one has

\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi=\pm\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\varphi+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{\Omega_{\pm}}.

We note that item (ii) of the previous proposition is a version of the well-known Plemelj-Sokhotski formula, see for instance
[
2
, Lemma 3.3 (i)]
, where
\mathcal{C}_{z}
is represented as a singular boundary integral operator.

3.
Resolvent formula for
H_{\varepsilon}
and analysis of the associated integral operators

Throughout this section let
\varepsilon_{1}
be the number specified in Proposition
2.4
, so that
\iota
acts as a bijective map from
\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
to
\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Moreover, let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
,
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
,
G_{z}
be the function defined in (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), and
\Sigma
be a hypersurface satisfying Hypothesis
2.1
with associated Weingarten map
W
(see Definition
2.3
). This section is devoted to the study of the integral operators which formally act on
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon}(z)f(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})

(3.1a)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\cdot\det(I-\varepsilon sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds,

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})

(3.1b)

\displaystyle\hskip 120.0pt\cdot\det(I-\varepsilon sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds,

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}(z)u(t)(x_{\Sigma})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y)u(y)\,dy,

(3.1c)

for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
, a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
, and
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
. First, in Section
3.1
we define these operators rigorously and show their relation to the resolvent of the operator
H_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.8
). Then, in Section
3.2
we introduce and investigate a shift operator which plays an important role in the convergence analysis of
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
in Section
3.3
. Finally, in Section
3.4
we discuss the convergence of
\left(I+{B_{\varepsilon}(z)}Vq\right)^{-1}
.

3.1.
Definition and elementary results on
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)

First, we rigorously define the operators
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
formally given by (
3.1
). Recall that
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
was defined in (
1.4
) and introduce the mappings

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{C}^{N}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}f(x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})):=f(t)(x_{\Sigma}),

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}u(t)(x_{\Sigma}):=u(x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})),

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}g(t):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}g\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right),

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}:L^{2}((-\varepsilon,\varepsilon);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}g(t):=\sqrt{\varepsilon}g(\varepsilon t).

According to Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
2.7
(iii) for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{1})
these mappings are well-defined, bounded, invertible, and their inverses have the claimed form, see also
[
4
, equations (3.6) and (3.7)]
.
Moreover, set
u_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
, where
\chi_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}
is the characteristic function for
\Omega_{\varepsilon}
, and define the operators

U_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{C}^{N})\quad\text{and}\quad U_{\varepsilon}^{*}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})

acting on
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
v\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as

U_{\varepsilon}u=(u_{\varepsilon}u)\upharpoonright\Omega_{\varepsilon}\quad\text{and}\quad U_{\varepsilon}^{*}v=\begin{cases}u_{\varepsilon}v&\text{ in }\Omega_{\varepsilon},\\
0&\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Omega_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Recall that we use the notation
R_{z}=(H-z)^{-1}
for the resolvent of the free Dirac operator
H
given by (
2.8
). Then, we define

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon}(z):=R_{z}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:L^{2}\big((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),

(3.4)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon}(z):=\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{z}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle:L^{2}\big((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big)\to L^{2}\big((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big),

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon}(z):=\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{z}

\displaystyle:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}\big((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\big).

Note that by definition these operators are well-defined and bounded. In the next proposition we show that these operators coincide with the formal expressions in (
3.1
).

Proposition 3.1
.

For the operators
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
defined in (
3.4
) the representations in (
3.1
) hold.

Proof.

First, we show the claim for
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
. Let
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Using (
2.9
), Proposition
2.4
(iii), (
3.2
), and (
3.3
) we find

\begin{split}A_{\varepsilon}&(z)f(x)=\left(R_{z}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f\right)(x)\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x-y)\left(U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f\right)(y)\,dy\\
&=\int_{\Omega_{\varepsilon}}G_{z}(x-y)u_{\varepsilon}(y)\left(\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f\right)(y)\,dy\\
&=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-(y_{\Sigma}+s\nu(y_{\Sigma})))\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\left(\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f\right)(y_{\Sigma}+s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\\
&\hskip 200.0pt\cdot\det(I-sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds\\
&=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma}-s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\frac{1}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}f)(s)(y_{\Sigma})\det(I-sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds\\
&=\int_{-\varepsilon}^{\varepsilon}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma}-s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\frac{1}{{\varepsilon}}f\!\left(\frac{s}{\varepsilon}\right)\!(y_{\Sigma})\det(I-sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds\\
&=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\det(I-\varepsilon sW(y_{\Sigma}))\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds\end{split}

for a.e.
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
, which is the claimed identity.
Next, to prove the claim for
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
we note for
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
, and
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
that

\begin{split}C_{\varepsilon}(z)v(t)(x_{\Sigma})&=\bigl(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v\bigr)(t)(x_{\Sigma})\\
&=\sqrt{\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v)(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma}))\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y)v(y)\,dy.\end{split}

The representation for
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
follows by combining the last two calculations.
∎

Next, we show a resolvent formula for
H_{\varepsilon}
involving the operators
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
which will be useful for the convergence analysis. Using the identifications in the end of Section
2.2
we regard
Vq
in the following as a
multiplication operator in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. A similar formula is shown in
[
30
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
be given by (
3.4
), let
q
and
V
be given by (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, and let
H_{\varepsilon}
be given by (
1.8
). If
-1\in\rho(B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)
, then
z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})
and

(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}=R_{z}-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z).

Proof.

Let
V_{\varepsilon}
be given by (
1.7
) and set
v_{\varepsilon}:=\sqrt{\varepsilon}V_{\varepsilon}
. Then,
H_{\varepsilon}=H+v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}
and
v_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}=u_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}Vq
. Hence, due to the invertibility of
I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq
and (
3.4
) we obtain

\begin{split}(H_{\varepsilon}&-z)\bigl(R_{z}-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\left(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\right)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z)\bigr)\\
&=(H-z+v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon})\bigl(R_{z}-R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\left(I+u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}\bigr)\\
&=I+v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}-v_{\varepsilon}\left(I+u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}-v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\left(I+u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}\\
&=I+v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}-v_{\varepsilon}\left(I+u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}+v_{\varepsilon}\left(I+u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}v_{\varepsilon}\right)^{-1}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}-v_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}R_{z}\\
&=I.\end{split}

A similar calculation shows that

\begin{split}\bigl(R_{z}-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\left(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\right)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z)\bigr)(H_{\varepsilon}-z)=I\end{split}

is true. The latter two equations imply the claim of this proposition.
∎

3.2.
The shift operator

In this subsection we introduce and study a shift operator which turns out to be useful in the convergence analysis of the maps
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
in (
3.4
). For that, we first construct a suitable extension of the normal vector field
\nu
on
\Sigma
. Let
\varphi_{\nu}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})
with
\varphi_{\nu}(0)=1
and compact support in
(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1})
, where
\varepsilon_{1}
is the number specified in Proposition
2.4
. Since
\Sigma
is assumed to satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
, it is not difficult to show that

\widetilde{\nu}(x)=\begin{cases}\nu(x_{\Sigma})\varphi_{\nu}(t),&\text{if }x=x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}\text{ with }(x_{\Sigma},t)\in\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\\
0,&\text{if }x\notin\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}},\end{cases}

is a
C^{1}
extension of
\nu
which is supported in
\Omega_{\varepsilon_{1}}
. For the sake of readability we use also the symbol
\nu
when referring to the extension
\widetilde{\nu}
from now on.

Next, we define for
\delta\in\mathbb{R}
the shift operator
\tau_{\delta}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\tau_{\delta}u(x):=u\left(x+\delta\nu(x)\right),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

(3.5)

In the next proposition we study basic properties of
\tau_{\delta}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
D\nu
be the Jacobi matrix of
\nu
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}^{-1})
. Then, for any
r\in[0,1]
the operators
\tau_{\delta}
,
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
, are uniformly bounded in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and for
r^{\prime}\in[0,r]

{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}-I}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\lvert{\delta}\right\rvert^{r-r^{\prime}}

(3.6)

holds for all
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
, where
C>0
is independent of
\delta
.

Proof.

Fix
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
and observe first that
I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x)
is invertible for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
and
the norm of the inverse is bounded by
(1-\left\lvert{\delta_{0}}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})})^{-1}
.
The same bound holds for the modulus of the eigenvalues of
(I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x))^{-1}
and hence we conclude

\left\lvert{\det\big((I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x))^{-1}\big)}\right\rvert\leq\frac{1}{\big(1-\left\lvert{\delta_{0}}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}\big)^{\theta}},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}.

(3.7)

We start by showing the uniform boundedness of
\tau_{\delta}
for
r=0
. Let
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then, change of variables and (
3.7
)
lead to

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}&\left\lvert{\tau_{\delta}u(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{u(x+\delta\nu(x))}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{u(x+\delta\nu(x))}\right\rvert^{2}\frac{\left\lvert{\det\left(I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x)\right)}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert{\det\left(I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x)\right)}\right\rvert}\,dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{u(x+\delta\nu(x))}\right\rvert^{2}\left\lvert{\det\left(I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x)\right)}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\det\big((I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x))^{-1}\big)}\right\rvert\,dx\\
&\leq\frac{1}{{\big(1-\left\lvert{\delta_{0}}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}\big)^{\theta}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{u(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx,\end{split}

(3.8)

and it follows that the operators
\tau_{\delta}
,
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
, are uniformly bounded
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. To see the uniform boundedness of the operators
\tau_{\delta}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, let
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and compute in a similar way as above

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{D(\tau_{\delta}u)(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{(Du)(x+\delta\nu(x))(I_{\theta}+\delta D\nu(x))}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx\\
&\leq\frac{\big(1+\delta_{0}{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}\big)^{2}}{{\big(1-\left\lvert{\delta_{0}}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}\big)^{\theta}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{Du(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx.\end{split}

(3.9)

By density this estimate remains valid for
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Therefore, the uniform boundedness of the operators
\tau_{\delta}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
follows from (
3.8
) and (
3.9
). Eventually, using interpolation one concludes that
\tau_{\delta}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
for any
r\in[0,1]
.

It remains to prove (
3.6
). Since we already have shown that
\tau_{\delta}
is uniformly bounded in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, the claim in (
3.6
) holds for
r=r^{\prime}\in[0,1]
. Next, we show (
3.6
) for
r^{\prime}=0
and
r=1
. With the main theorem of calculus and the chain rule we find for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{\tau_{\delta}u(x)-u(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{\delta}\frac{d}{dt}u(x+t\nu(x))dt}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left\lvert{\int_{0}^{\delta}Du(x+t\nu(x))\nu(x)dt}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx\\
&\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}\left(\int_{0}^{\delta}\left\lvert{(\tau_{t}Du)(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dt\right)\left(\int_{0}^{\delta}\left\lvert{\nu(x)}\right\rvert^{2}\,dt\right)\,dx\\
&\leq|\delta|{\left\lVert{\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta})}^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}{\left\lVert{\tau_{t}Du}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times\theta})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C|\delta|\int_{0}^{\delta}{\left\lVert{Du}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N\times\theta})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\left\lvert{\delta}\right\rvert^{2}{\left\lVert{u}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2},\end{split}

where
\tau_{t}Du
is understood column-wise. By density this estimate remains valid for
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and hence

{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}-I}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\lvert{\delta}\right\rvert
.
It remains to prove the claim in the case
0\leq r^{\prime}<r\leq 1
with
(r^{\prime},r)\neq(0,1)
. We set
\mu=r-r^{\prime}\in(0,1)
and
\upsilon=\frac{r^{\prime}}{1-(r-r^{\prime})}\in[0,1]
. Then,

r^{\prime}=(1-\mu)\upsilon+\mu 0\quad\text{and}\quad r=(1-\mu)\upsilon+\mu 1

and consequently
[
32
, Theorem B.7]
implies

H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})=[H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{\mu}=[H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{\mu}

and

H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})=[H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{\mu}.

Applying (
1.18
) yields

\begin{split}&{\left\lVert{I-\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\hskip 20.0pt\leq C{\left\lVert{I-\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{[H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{\mu}\to[H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{\mu}}\\
&\hskip 20.0pt\leq C{\left\lVert{I-\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{\upsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{1-\mu}{\left\lVert{I-\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{\mu}\\
&\hskip 20.0pt=C\left\lvert{\delta}\right\rvert^{r-r^{\prime}},\end{split}

which is exactly (
3.6
). This finishes the proof of this proposition.
∎

We will also need a variant of the shift operator
\tau_{\delta}
that acts on functions defined on
\Omega_{\pm}
.
Since
\Omega_{\pm}
satisfy Hypothesis
2.1

we can make use of Stein’s extension operator
E:L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
which has the properties
(Ef)_{\pm}=f
for
f\in L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and which has a continuous restriction
E:H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
for any
r\geq 0
,
see
[
40
, Chapter 6, Section 3, Theorem 5]
.
We then define the shift operator for functions on
\Omega_{\pm}
by

\tau_{\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}:=(\tau_{\delta}E(\cdot))_{\pm}:L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(3.10)

The following properties of
\tau_{\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}
follow immediately from the properties of
E
and Proposition
3.3
.

Corollary 3.4
.

Let
D\nu
be the Jacobi matrix of
\nu
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}^{-1})
. Then, for any
r\in[0,1]
the operators
\tau_{\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}
,
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
, are uniformly bounded in
H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and for
r^{\prime}\in[0,r]

{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}-I}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{r^{\prime}}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\lvert{\delta}\right\rvert^{r-r^{\prime}}

holds for all
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
, where
C>0
is independent of
\delta
.

Eventually, we show that the map
t\mapsto\tau_{t\delta}u
has a useful continuity property.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
D\nu
be the Jacobi matrix of
\nu
,
\delta_{0}\in(0,{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}^{-1})
,
\delta\in[-\delta_{0},\delta_{0}]
,
r\in[0,1]
,
u\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
v\in H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then, the functions

\begin{split}f_{u}:(-1,1)&\to H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}),\qquad t\mapsto\tau_{t\delta}u,\end{split}

and

\begin{split}f_{v}^{\pm}:(-1,1)&\to H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm}\;\mathbb{C}^{N}),\qquad t\mapsto\tau_{t\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}v,\end{split}

are continuous.

Proof.

First, consider
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and let
t_{n},t\in(-1,1)
such that
t_{n}\rightarrow t
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Then, with dominated convergence one gets

\lim_{n\to\infty}f_{u}(t_{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}u((\cdot)+\delta t_{n}\nu)=u((\cdot)+\delta t\nu)=f_{u}(t)\quad\textup{in }H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Since
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
is continuously embedded in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, the assertion follows for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. If
u\in H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, then there exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that
u_{n}\to u
in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as
n\to\infty
. Applying Proposition
3.3
yields

\begin{split}{\left\lVert{f_{u}(t)-f_{u_{n}}(t)}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}&={\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta t}(u-u_{n})}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C{\left\lVert{u-u_{n}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\end{split}

for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in(-1,1)
. Hence,
f_{u_{n}}(t)\to f_{u}(t)
uniformly with respect to
t
in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,
f_{u}
is also continuous.

It remains to verify the claim for
f_{v}^{\pm}
. Let
t_{n},t\in(-1,1)
such that
t_{n}\rightarrow t
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Using the properties of Stein’s extension operator
E
and the above observations, we get that
f_{Ev}(t_{n})\rightarrow f_{Ev}(t)
in
H^{r}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Moreover, the boundedness of the restriction mapping gives us that

f_{v}^{\pm}(t_{n})=\left(\tau_{\delta t_{n}}Ev\right)_{\pm}=\left(f_{Ev}(t_{n})\right)_{\pm}
converges to
\left(f_{Ev}(t)\right)_{\pm}=f_{v}^{\pm}(t)
in
H^{r}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. This shows the continuity of
f_{v}^{\pm}
.
∎

3.3.
Convergence of
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)

This section is devoted to the convergence analysis of the operators
A_{\varepsilon}(z),B_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
introduced in (
3.4
) for
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0+
. First, in Proposition
3.7
we study the convergence of
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
, which allows us with a duality argument to investigate the convergence of
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
in Proposition
3.8
. Eventually, in Proposition
3.10
we consider the convergence of
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
.

We choose the number
\varepsilon_{2}>0
such that

\varepsilon_{2}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{1}{2{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}}\right\},

(3.11)

where
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{1}
are specified in Hypothesis
2.1
and Proposition
2.4
, respectively.
Let
W
be the Weingarten map associated with
\Sigma
introduced in Definition
2.3
. In our analysis, the operator
M_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
acting as

\begin{split}M_{\varepsilon}f(t)=\det\left(I-t\varepsilon W\right)f(t)\qquad\text{ for a.e. }t\in(-1,1)\end{split}

(3.12)

will be useful. In the following lemma, which is an immediate consequence of Proposition
2.4
(ii), some relevant properties of
M_{\varepsilon}
are stated.

Lemma 3.6
.

For any
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}]
the operator
M_{\varepsilon}
is bounded, invertible,

{\left\lVert{M_{\varepsilon}}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0}\leq(1+\varepsilon C)\quad\text{and}\quad{\left\lVert{M_{\varepsilon}-I}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0}\leq\varepsilon C.

To formulate the result about the convergence of
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
, recall that the embedding
\mathfrak{J}
is defined in (
2.7
) and introduce the operator

\begin{split}C_{0}(z):=\mathfrak{J}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}:L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})).\end{split}

(3.13)

In fact, the properties of
\mathfrak{J}
and
\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}
, see (
2.7
) and Proposition
2.8
, imply that
C_{0}(z)
gives rise also to a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
R_{z}=(H-z)^{-1}
,
\tau_{(\cdot)}
be the shift operator in (
3.5
), and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
satisfying (
3.11
). Then, the relation

C_{\varepsilon}(z)u(t)=\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u\qquad\text{ for a.e. }t\in(-1,1)

(3.14)

holds in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
\textup{ran }C_{\varepsilon}(z)\subset L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Moreover, the operators

C_{\varepsilon}(z)
are uniformly bounded from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and for any
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
one has
