{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to 0}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.

(3.15)

Proof.

First, we show (
3.14
) for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. By density and continuity, this implies (
3.14
) for all
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Recall that
R_{z}:H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{s+1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
is bounded for
s\in\mathbb{R}
; cf. Section
2.3
. Hence, by the Sobolev embedding theorem
R_{z}u
is continuous for
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the same is true for
\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u
.
Furthermore, as
\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
we conclude with Proposition
3.1
for
t\in(-1,1)
and
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
that

\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u(x_{\Sigma})=\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta}}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y)u(y)dy=(C_{\varepsilon}(z)u)(t)(x_{\Sigma}).

Hence, (
3.14
) is true.

Next, we show the inclusion
\textup{ran }C_{\varepsilon}(z)\subset L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Assume that
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then,
by Proposition
3.5
and the boundedness of the trace
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}:H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
it follows that the function
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{(\cdot)\varepsilon}R_{z}u
is continuous as a mapping from
(-1,1)
to
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{(\cdot)\varepsilon}R_{z}u
is measurable as a mapping from
(-1,1)
to
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Using again the boundedness of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}:H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and the uniform boundedness of the shift operator in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see Propositions
2.2
and
3.3
, respectively, we conclude

\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{\varepsilon t}R_{z}u}\right\rVert}^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,dt\leq\int_{-1}^{1}C{\left\lVert{R_{z}u}\right\rVert}^{2}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,dt\leq C{\left\lVert{u}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}

and therefore
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{(\cdot)\varepsilon}R_{z}u\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Moreover, this also shows that
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
is uniformly bounded from
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

Eventually, with Proposition
3.3
and the identity
C_{0}(z)=\mathfrak{J}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}=\mathfrak{J}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}R_{z}
, see Proposition
2.8
(iii), we have for
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
and
u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})

\begin{split}{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(z)u-C_{0}(z)u}\right\rVert}_{0}^{2}&={\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}\tau_{\varepsilon(\cdot)}R_{z}u-\mathfrak{J}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}R_{z}u}\right\rVert}_{0}^{2}\\
&=\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}(\tau_{\varepsilon t}-I)R_{z}u}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{(\tau_{\varepsilon t}-I)R_{z}u}\right\rVert}_{H^{r+1/2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon t}\right\rvert^{1-2r}{\left\lVert{R_{z}u}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}\varepsilon^{1-2r}{\left\lVert{u}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1-2r}{\left\lVert{u}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2},\end{split}

which leads to (
3.15
). Therefore, all claims are shown.
∎

Using the convergence of
C_{\varepsilon}(z)
, it is not difficult to show the convergence of
A_{\varepsilon}(z)
. We define the natural candidate for the limit operator by

A_{0}(z):=\Phi_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N}).

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
satisfying (
3.11
). Then, for any

r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
one has

{\left\lVert{A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}

and, in particular, the operators
A_{\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
are uniformly bounded.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
,
S_{\varepsilon}
, and
M_{\varepsilon}
be the operators given by (
3.2
), (
3.3
), and (
3.12
), respectively. One verifies by a direct calculation using Proposition
2.4
(iii), (
3.2
), and (
3.3
) that
(\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})^{*}=M_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
. Using this relation we conclude from (
3.4
) that

\begin{split}(A_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}^{-1})^{*}&=M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(A_{\varepsilon}(z))^{*}=M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(R_{z}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})^{*}\\
&=M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}M_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{\overline{z}}=\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{\overline{z}}=C_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}).\end{split}

Moreover,
(A_{0}(z))^{*}=(\Phi_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*})^{*}=\mathfrak{J}\Phi_{z}^{*}=C_{0}(\overline{z})
. Hence, Lemma
3.6
and Proposition
3.7
yield

\begin{split}\big\|A_{\varepsilon}(z)&-A_{0}(z)\big\|_{0\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left\lVert{A_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(M_{\varepsilon}-I)+A_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}-A_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to 0}+{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})-C_{0}(\overline{z})}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to 0}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\end{split}

which is the claimed estimate.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

For completeness we note that one can follow the proof of Proposition
3.7
to show

{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to r}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}

for
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
.
Moreover, if
\Sigma
is
C^{3}
smooth, one can extend the result of Lemma
3.6
to spaces
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{t}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
,
t\in[-1,1]
, and use this to verify

{\left\lVert{A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{-r\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}

for
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
in a similar way as in the proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Next, we study the convergence of the operators
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
. Define the limit operator by
B_{0}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
which acts on
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
evaluated for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
as

\begin{split}B_{0}(z)f(t)&:=\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f(s)\,ds+\mathcal{C}_{z}\int_{-1}^{1}f(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(3.16)

where
\mathcal{C}_{z}:L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
is the extension of the operator defined in (
2.14
) from Proposition
2.9
. Using the mapping properties of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
in Proposition
2.9
(i) it follows that

B_{0}(z)
can also be regarded as an operator in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
for any
r\in\bigl[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}\bigr]
.
In the following proposition we show that
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
converges to
B_{0}(z)
. The proof of this result is more complicated as the proofs of Proposition
3.7
and Proposition
3.8
, and therefore some of the more technical calculations are shifted to Appendix
B
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
satisfying (
3.11
). Then,
the operators
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
are uniformly bounded in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
for any
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
one has

{\left\lVert{B_{\varepsilon}(z)-B_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.

Proof.

The proof is split into several steps. Let
\Phi_{z}
be as in (
2.12
) and let
\tau_{(\cdot)}^{\Omega_{\pm}}
be defined by (
3.10
). We introduce the auxiliary operators

\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z):=B_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),

(3.17)

which are, due to the properties of
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
and
M_{\varepsilon}
in (
3.4
) and (
3.12
), bounded and
act on
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
evaluated at
t\in(-1,1)
and
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
as

\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds.

(3.18)

Moreover, we define

\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

acting on
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
as

\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)=\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}ds+\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}\,ds.

(3.19)

First, in
Step 1
we show that
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is bounded and converges to
B_{0}(z)
, then in
Step 2
we verify an alternative representation of
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
. In
Step 3
we use Appendix
B
to compare
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
and
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
, and show that
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is uniformly bounded in
\varepsilon
. In
Step 4
we combine the results from
Step 1
to
Step 3
to conclude the claim of this proposition.

Step 1.
First, we note that, due to Definition
2.5
and Proposition
3.5
, the function
(-1,1)^{2}\ni(t,s)\mapsto\Theta(\mp(t-s))\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{\pm}}\in\mathcal{L}(H^{1}(\Omega_{\pm}),H^{1}(\Omega_{\pm}))
is measurable, where
\Theta
is the Heaviside function. Hence, it follows with Definition
2.5
that the integrands in (
3.19
) are measurable with respect to
(t,s)\in(-1,1)^{2}
. Moreover, by the mapping properties of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}
,
\tau_{(\cdot)}^{\Omega_{\pm}}
, and
\Phi_{z}
in Proposition
2.2
, Corollary
3.4
, and Proposition
2.8
, respectively, the integrands are bounded by
C{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
for
(t,s)\in(-1,1)^{2}
. In particular, we conclude that for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{-1}^{1}\big\|\Theta(t-s)\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}+\Theta(s-t)\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}\big\|^{2}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}dtds

is finite.
Thus, Fubini’s theorem for Bochner integrals, cf. Section
2.2
, yields the integrability of the integrands in (
3.19
) with respect to
s\in(-1,1)
and the measurability of
t\mapsto\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)
. Furthermore, the bound for the integrands implies also
{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f}\right\rVert}_{1/2}\leq C{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{1/2}
for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Hence,
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is well defined and uniformly bounded in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. We claim that

{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-{B}_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\leq C{\varepsilon}^{1/2-r}.

(3.20)

To see this we remark that with Proposition
2.9
(ii) we have the pointwise representation

B_{0}(z)f(t)=\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\left(\Phi_{z}f(s)\right)_{-}\,ds+\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\left(\Phi_{z}f(s)\right)_{+}\,ds

(3.21)

for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
and
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.
Thus,
r\in\big(0,\tfrac{1}{2}\big)
and direct estimates show

\begin{split}{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f-{B}_{0}(z)f}\right\rVert}_{0}^{2}&=\int_{-1}^{1}\left\|\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\bigl(\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}-I\bigr)(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}ds\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad+\left.\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\bigl(\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}-I\bigr)(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}ds\,\right\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,dt\\
&\hskip-60.0pt\leq\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{-1}^{t}{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\bigl(\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}-I\bigr)(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}ds\right.\\
&\left.+\int_{t}^{1}{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\bigl(\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}-I\bigr)(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}ds\right)^{2}\,dt.\end{split}

(3.22)

Employing Proposition
2.2
, Corollary
3.4
, and Proposition
2.8
yields for all
s,t\in(-1,1)

{\left\lVert{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\big(\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{\pm}}-I\big)(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{\pm}}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}.

Plugging this into (
3.22
) we obtain

\begin{split}\big\|\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}&(z)f-{B}_{0}(z)f\big\|_{0}^{2}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{2(1/2-r)}\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{-1}^{t}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds+\int_{t}^{1}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds\right)^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{2(1/2-r)}\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,ds=C\varepsilon^{2(1/2-r)}{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{1/2}^{2},\end{split}

which implies (
3.20
).

Step 2.
We show that the operator
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in (
3.19
) has the alternative representation

\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma})f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds.

(3.23)

Let
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
t,s\in(-1,1)
be fixed such that
t>s
. Note that the choice of
\varepsilon_{1}
in Proposition
2.4
implies
\varepsilon_{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{1}}{2}<\frac{\varepsilon_{A}}{2}
, cf. also Proposition
A.2
. Hence, by Corollary
A.3
we have

x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Omega_{-}
for all
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
. Moreover, we conclude from the representation of
\Phi_{z}
given in (
2.12
) and the form of the integral kernel
G_{z}
, see (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), that
\Phi_{z}f(s)
is continuous away from
\Sigma
. Thus, we have for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}(x_{\Sigma})&=(\Phi_{z}f(s))(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma}))\\
&=\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma})f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Analogously, for
t<s
and
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}(x_{\Sigma})&=(\Phi_{z}f(s))(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma}))\\
&=\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma})f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Combining the previous two equations yields

\begin{split}&\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}(x_{\Sigma})\,ds+\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}(x_{\Sigma})\,ds\\
&\hskip 80.0pt=\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma})f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds.\end{split}

(3.24)

Moreover, as the integrands on the right hand side in (
3.19
) are Bochner integrable (cf.
Step 1
), Proposition
2.7
(iii) shows that the pointwise evaluation of the Bochner integrals in the definition of
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in (
3.19
) coincides with (
3.24
), i.e.

\begin{split}&\bigg(\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}\left(\Phi_{z}f(s)\right)_{-}\,ds+\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}\left(\Phi_{z}f(s)\right)_{+}\,ds\bigg)(x_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 40.0pt=\int_{-1}^{t}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}(x_{\Sigma})\,ds+\int_{t}^{1}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}(x_{\Sigma})\,ds.\end{split}

This is exactly the claimed formula in (
3.23
).

Step 3.
By the results in Appendix
B
the map

\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
admits an extension to a bounded operator from
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and

\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{0\to 1/2}\leq C(\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert)^{1/2}.

(3.25)

Moreover, we claim that
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. To see this, observe first that

\begin{split}\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 1/2}&\leq\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 1/2}+\big\|\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 1/2}\\
&\leq\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{0\to 1/2}+\big\|\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 1/2}.\end{split}

(3.26)

Therefore, the estimate (
3.25
) and the uniform boundedness of the operators
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

shown in
Step 1
imply that
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
is also uniformly bounded in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.
The same is true for
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})
and the hence also the anti-dual

(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}:L^{2}\bigl((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\bigr)\to L^{2}\bigl((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\bigr)

is uniformly bounded. We claim that
(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}

is an extension of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
, that is,

\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f=(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}f,\qquad f\in L^{2}\bigl((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\bigr).

(3.27)

In fact,
the identities
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})=B_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, (
3.4
), and
(\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon})^{*}=M_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
, yield for the adjoint of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})
in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{*}=(\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{\overline{z}}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}M_{\varepsilon}^{-1})^{*}=\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}U_{\varepsilon}R_{z}U_{\varepsilon}^{*}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}\mathcal{S}_{\varepsilon}M_{\varepsilon}^{-1}=\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)

and hence
Proposition
2.7
(ii) implies for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
g\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

\begin{split}\big\langle(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}f&,g\big\rangle_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\times L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\\
&=\big\langle f,\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})g\big\rangle_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\times L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\\
&=\big(f,\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})g\big)_{L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\\
&=\big(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f,g\big)_{L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}\\
&=\big\langle\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f,g\big\rangle_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\times L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))},\end{split}

where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\times L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))}
denotes the sesquilinear duality product, which is anti-linear in the second argument.
This implies (
3.27
) and since
(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
are both uniformly bounded
in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, respectively, an interpolation argument leads to the uniform boundedness of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

Step 4.
Using the results from
Step 1
to
Step 3
we will now complete the proof of Proposition
3.10
. Since
B_{\varepsilon}(z)=\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}
, Lemma
3.6
and the uniform boundedness of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
shown in
Step 3
imply the uniform boundedness of
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, proving the first claim of this proposition. Moreover, by applying Lemma
3.6
, (
3.25
), (
3.20
), and the uniform boundedness of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in the space
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
we obtain

\begin{split}\big\|B_{\varepsilon}&(z)-B_{0}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 0}={\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)M_{\varepsilon}-{B}_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\leq{\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)(M_{\varepsilon}-I)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}+{\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}+{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-{B}_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\leq{\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)(M_{\varepsilon}-I)}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0}+{\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to 1/2}+{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-{B}_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\leq C\bigl(\varepsilon+(\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert)^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{1/2-r}\bigr)\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.\end{split}

This is the claimed norm estimate and finishes the proof of this proposition.
∎

3.4.
Convergence of
\left(I+{B_{\varepsilon}(z)}Vq\right)^{-1}

Recall that
B_{\varepsilon}(z)
is defined in (
3.4
) and let
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively. In this section we treat the convergence of
\left(I+{B_{\varepsilon}(z)}Vq\right)^{-1}
under suitable assumptions on
q
and
V
.
The crucial assumption here, which is also needed in our main result Theorem
1.1
, is that
Vq
is bounded by

X_{z}:=\frac{1}{\max\{\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{B_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0},{\left\lVert{B_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 1/2}\}},

(3.28)

where
\varepsilon_{2}
is chosen such that (
3.11
) is satisfied and
z\in\rho(H)
is fixed. After the convergence result in the next proposition we
provide more information on
X_{z}
in Lemma
3.13
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
satisfying (
3.11
), and let
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, such that

{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}<X_{z}.

(3.29)

Then,
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}
and
(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}
give rise to bounded operators from
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
to
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
for any
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
one has

{\left\lVert{(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}-(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.

Remark 3.12
.

Note that assumption (
3.29
) implies the following:

(i)

The operators
I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq
are bijective in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
for
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
and
their inverses are uniformly bounded.

(ii)

The operator
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is bijective in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

The statement of Proposition
3.11
remains true, if (
3.29
) is replaced by the weaker assumptions (i) and (ii).

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

Since
V
and
q
fulfil (
3.29
) we have

\begin{split}\big((I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}&-(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}\big)f\\
&=(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(z)-B_{0}(z))Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}f\end{split}

for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
; cf. Remark
3.12
.
Thus, with Proposition
3.10
and the fact that
Vq

induces a bounded operators in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, see (
2.3
) and Section
2.2
, we find

\begin{split}&{\left\lVert{(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}-(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\hskip 5.0pt\leq{\left\lVert{(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0}{\left\lVert{(B_{0}(z)-B_{\varepsilon}(z))Vq}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}{\left\lVert{(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 1/2}\\
&\hskip 5.0pt\leq C{\left\lVert{B_{0}(z)-B_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\hskip 5.0pt\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\end{split}

which is the claimed result.
∎

In the following lemma we give more explicit estimates for the constant
X_{z}
in (
3.28
). Recall that
E
denotes Stein’s extension operator. Moreover, we introduce
C_{1/2,-1/2}:=\frac{C_{2}}{C_{1}}\geq 1
,
where
C_{2}\geq C_{1}>0
are chosen such that

C_{1}{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{0}\leq{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{[L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))]_{1/2}}\leq C_{2}{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{0}

holds for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
; note that according to Proposition
2.7
(i) such a choice is possible.
If
B
is a bounded operator in
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
that admits a bounded extension to

L^{2}((-1,1);H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and a bounded restriction to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
, then
Proposition
2.7
(i) and (
1.18
) imply

\|B\|_{0\rightarrow 0}\leq C_{1/2,-1/2}\|B\|_{1/2\rightarrow 1/2}^{1/2}\|B\|_{-1/2\rightarrow-1/2}^{1/2}.

(3.30)

Lemma 3.13
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and let
X_{z}
be as in (
3.28
). Then,
the following assertions hold.

(i)

X_{z}\geq\frac{1}{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\left\lvert{\log\varepsilon_{2}}\right\rvert\varepsilon_{2}})
, where the constant

\begin{split}C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}&=2C_{1/2,-1/2}\max_{(\textup{sign},\mu)\in\{+,-\}\times\{z,\overline{z}\}}\big\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\textup{sign}}\big\|_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\textup{sign}};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\qquad\quad\cdot{\left\lVert{E}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\textup{sign}};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}{\left\lVert{\Phi_{\mu}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\Omega_{\textup{sign}};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\end{split}

depends only on the geometry of
\Sigma
and
z\in\rho(H)
.

(ii)

If
\Sigma
is
C^{\infty}
-smooth and compact and
m>0
, then
X_{z}\leq\frac{\pi}{4}
.

In particular, if
{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}<\frac{1}{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
is chosen sufficiently small, then the claim in Proposition
3.11
is true.

Proof.

(i) First, by the definition of
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
,
\tau_{\delta}^{\Omega_{\pm}}
in (
3.19
) and (
3.10
), respectively, and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality we have for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})

\begin{split}&{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)f}\right\rVert}_{1/2}^{2}\leq\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{-1}^{t}\big\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{-}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{-}\big\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}ds\right.\\
&\hskip 100.0pt\left.+\int_{t}^{1}\big\|\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\tau_{\varepsilon(t-s)}^{\Omega_{+}}(\Phi_{z}f(s))_{+}\big\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}ds\right)^{2}\,dt\\
&\leq\!\int_{-1}^{1}\!\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\frac{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}{2C_{1/2,-1/2}}\!\sup_{\delta\in(-2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{2})}\!\!{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}ds\right)^{2}\!dt\\
&\leq\left(\frac{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}{C_{1/2,-1/2}}\sup_{\delta\in(-2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{1/2}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

Hence,

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 1/2}\leq\frac{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}{C_{1/2,-1/2}}\sup_{\delta\in(-2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}

and a similar calculation using (
3.21
), the estimate
{\left\lVert{E}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\Omega_{\textup{sign}};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\geq 1
, and
C_{1/2,-1/2}\geq 1
leads to

{\left\lVert{\overline{B}_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 1/2}\leq C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}.

(3.31)

Let us proceed with the estimate for
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
. Since by (
3.8
) and (
3.9
)

\sup_{\delta\in(-2\varepsilon_{2},2\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{\tau_{\delta}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq\frac{1+2\varepsilon_{2}{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}}{{\big(1-2\varepsilon_{2}{\left\lVert{D\nu}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta})}\big)^{\theta/2}}}=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon_{2}),

the results from (
3.25
)–(
3.26
) and Appendix
B
imply

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{1/2\to 1/2}\leq\frac{C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}}{C_{1/2,-1/2}}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\left\lvert{\log\varepsilon_{2}}\right\rvert\varepsilon_{2}}).

(3.32)

It is clear that the estimates (
3.31
) and (
3.32
) hold also with
z
replaced by
\overline{z}
.
As in
Step 3
of the proof of Proposition
3.10
we use again that the anti-dual
(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})

in
L^{2}((-1,1)H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
is an extension of

\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
that is bounded by
\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})\|_{1/2\to 1/2}
. Hence, with (
3.30
) one gets

\begin{split}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}\big\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\big\|_{0\to 0}&\leq\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}C_{1/2,-1/2}\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\|_{1/2\to 1/2}^{1/2}\|(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z}))^{\prime}\|_{-1/2\to-1/2}^{1/2}\\
&=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}C_{1/2,-1/2}\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\|_{1/2\to 1/2}^{1/2}\|\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(\overline{z})\|_{1/2\to 1/2}^{1/2}\\
&\leq C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\left\lvert{\log\varepsilon_{2}}\right\rvert\varepsilon_{2}}).\end{split}

Thus, it follows from the definition of
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
in (
3.17
) and Lemma
3.6
that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})}{\left\lVert{B_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to 0}\leq C_{\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma},E,z}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\left\lvert{\log\varepsilon_{2}}\right\rvert\varepsilon_{2}}).

Together with (
3.31
) this yields the claim in (i).

(ii) First, note that there exist
\varphi_{n}\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

{\left\lVert{\varphi_{n}}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}=1\quad\text{and}\quad\left\|\left(-\frac{1}{2}I_{N}+\mathcal{C}_{z}\right)\varphi_{n}\right\|_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\rightarrow 0,\quad\text{as }n\rightarrow\infty;

(3.33)

for
z\in(-m,m)
this follows from
[
23
, Corollaries 3.8 and 4.12]
,
while for
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}
one may additionally use that
[
10
, Proposition 4.4 (iv)]
implies that
\mathcal{C}_{z}-\mathcal{C}_{0}
is compact in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Define the functions
f_{n}\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
by
f_{n}(t):=\exp(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\pi t/4)\varphi_{n}
and compute with Proposition
2.7
(iii)

\begin{split}\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)&\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f_{n}(s)ds\\
&=\frac{2}{\pi}\left(\int_{-1}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}s\big)\varphi_{n}ds-\int_{t}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}s\big)\varphi_{n}ds\right)\\
&=\frac{4}{\pi}\left(\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}t\big)-\cos\big((\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}\big)\right)\varphi_{n}\\
&=\frac{4}{\pi}\left(f_{n}(t)-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}I_{N}\varphi_{n}\right),\end{split}

where in the last step the identity
\cos((\alpha\cdot\nu)\pi/4)=\cos(\pi/4)I_{N}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}I_{N}
, which can be shown via the power series representation of the cosine function and
(\alpha\cdot\nu)^{2}=I_{N}
, was used. With a similar argument, one verifies that
(\alpha\cdot\nu)\sin((\alpha\cdot\nu)\pi/4)=\sin(\pi/4)I_{N}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}I_{N}
, which yields

\begin{split}\int_{-1}^{1}f_{n}(s)ds&=\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}s\big)\varphi_{n}ds=\frac{8}{\pi}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\sin\big((\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{\pi}{4}\big)\varphi_{n}=\frac{4\sqrt{2}}{\pi}\varphi_{n}.\end{split}

Combining the last two displayed formulas with the representation of
B_{0}(z)
in (
3.16
), we conclude

\begin{split}B_{0}(z)f_{n}(t)&=\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f(s)\,ds+\mathcal{C}_{z}\int_{-1}^{1}f(s)\,ds\\
&=\frac{4}{\pi}\left(f_{n}(t)+\sqrt{2}\left(-\frac{1}{2}I_{N}+\mathcal{C}_{z}\right)\varphi_{n}\right).\end{split}

Hence, using (
3.33
), we find that

\|B_{0}(z)\|_{1/2\to 1/2}\geq\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\|B_{0}(z)f_{n}\|_{1/2}}{\|f_{n}\|_{1/2}}=\frac{4}{\pi}

and thus
X_{z}\leq\tfrac{\pi}{4}
.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section we prove the main results of this paper. For this we show in Proposition
4.1

that the resolvent
(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}
of the self-adjoint operator
H_{\varepsilon}
in (
1.8
) converges to the limit
operator

\mathcal{L}(z):=R_{z}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)

(4.1)

whenever
q
and
V
in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, satisfy the condition (
3.29
) for some
z\in\rho(H)=\mathbb{C}\setminus((-\infty,-|m|]\cup[|m|,\infty))
.
Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.3
collect some auxiliary considerations that are needed in Proposition
4.4
,
where it is shown for the limit operator that

\mathcal{L}(z)=(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}
with
H_{\widetilde{V}}
defined by (
1.2
). After these preparations
we complete the proofs of Theorem
1.1
, Corollary
1.2
, and Corollary
1.3
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}
satisfying (
3.11
), and let
q
and
V
be
as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, such that (
3.29
) holds.
Then,
z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})
and for any
r\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2})
one has

\begin{split}&\|(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-\mathcal{L}(z)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r},\end{split}

where the operator
\mathcal{L}(z)
is given by (
4.1
).
In particular,
(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}
converges to
\mathcal{L}(z)
in the operator norm as
\varepsilon\to 0+
.

Proof.

By Proposition
3.2
and (
3.29
), see also Remark
3.12
, we have
z\in\rho(H_{\varepsilon})
and

\begin{split}(H_{\varepsilon}-&z)^{-1}-\mathcal{L}(z)=(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-R_{z}+A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)\\
=&-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{\varepsilon}(z)+A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)\\
=&-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}(C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z))\\
&-A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\big((I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}-(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}\big)C_{0}(z)\\
&-(A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z))Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z).\end{split}

(4.2)

In the following we use that
A_{\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
are uniformly bounded; cf. Proposition
3.8
and Remark
3.12
(i).
Employing this and Proposition
3.7
we see that

\begin{split}\bigl\|A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)&Vq)^{-1}(C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z))\bigr\|_{{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}}\\
&\quad\leq C{\left\lVert{C_{\varepsilon}(z)-C_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to 0}\\
&\quad\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.\end{split}

(4.3)

Since
C_{0}(z):L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
in (
3.13
) is bounded, Proposition
3.11
yields

\begin{split}&\big\|A_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq\left((I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}-(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}\right)C_{0}(z)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\quad\quad\leq C{\left\lVert{(I+B_{\varepsilon}(z)Vq)^{-1}-(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}}\right\rVert}_{1/2\to 0}\\
&\quad\quad\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.\end{split}

(4.4)

Eventually, in a similar way as in (
4.3
) we find with Proposition
3.8
that

\begin{split}&{\left\lVert{(A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z))Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C{\left\lVert{A_{\varepsilon}(z)-A_{0}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.\end{split}

(4.5)

Combining (
4.3
)–(
4.5
) with (
4.2
) shows the claim of this proposition.
∎

The next goal is to show that the limit operator
\mathcal{L}(z)
in (
4.1
)
is the resolvent of
H_{\widetilde{V}}
defined in (
1.2
). This requires some technical preparations and we
first introduce the operator

\begin{split}T&:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),\\
Tf(t)&:=\frac{i}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)f(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(4.6)

and the function

Q(t):=-\frac{1}{2}+\int_{-1}^{t}q(s)ds,\quad t\in[-1,1].

(4.7)

Note that
Q
satisfies
Q^{\prime}=q
,
Q(-1)=-\frac{1}{2}
, and by (
1.5
) also
Q(1)=\frac{1}{2}
. Moreover, for
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
the map
T
gives rise to a bounded operator in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, let
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, and assume that
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Then, the following is true:

(i)

I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq
is boundedly invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and its inverse is given by (
4.10
).

(ii)

If
f\in\textup{ran }\mathfrak{J}
(that is,
f
is independent of
t\in(-1,1)
), then

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}f(t)=\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ(t))f(t)

(4.8)

holds for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
.

Proof.

(i) It will be shown that the operator defined in (
4.10
) below is the inverse of
I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq
.
Fix
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
and define the operators

\begin{split}\Xi&:L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\rightarrow H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),\\
\Xi f&=\frac{1}{2}\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\big(Q(s)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\big)q(s)f(s)\,ds,\end{split}

(4.9)

and

\begin{split}O&:L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\\
Of(t)&:=f(t)+\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ(t))\Xi f\\
&\hskip 37.0pt-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\int_{-1}^{t}\exp(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V(Q(s)-Q(t)))q(s)f(s)\,ds.\end{split}

(4.10)

We will show that
\Xi
and
O
are bounded and that
O=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}
. First, we verify that
\Xi
is well-defined and bounded. Let

f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Then the integrand in (
4.9
) is measurable as a function from
(-1,1)
to
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})

since
{\left({\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\big(Q(\cdot)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\big)q(\cdot)f(\cdot)},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
is measurable for all
\psi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see Definition
2.5
. In fact, the latter function is the pointwise limit of the sequence of measurable functions

\begin{split}&t\mapsto\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{\big(\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\big(Q(t)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\big)^{k}q(t)f(t),\psi\big)_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}}{k!}\\
&\hskip 100.0pt=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{q(t)\big(Q(t)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)^{k}{\left({f(t)},{(-iV(\alpha\cdot\nu))^{k}\psi}\right)}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}}{k!}.\end{split}

Moreover, as
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1},\alpha\cdot\nu,V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})

it follows that

\begin{split}\|&\Xi f\|_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}{\left\lVert{\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\int_{-1}^{1}\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\big(Q(s)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\big)q(s)f(s)\,ds}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{\exp\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V\big(Q(s)-\tfrac{1}{2}\big)\big)q(s)f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds\\
\end{split}

and
\alpha\cdot\nu,V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
also imply

\exp(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)V(Q(s)-\tfrac{1}{2}))\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
via the
power series of the exponential function. Using
q\in L^{\infty}((-1,1);\mathbb{R})
we conclude

\begin{split}\|&\Xi f\|_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{\exp\big((\alpha\cdot\nu)V(Q(s)-\tfrac{1}{2})\big)}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}((-1,1);\mathbb{R})}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds\\
&\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{r}.\end{split}

This shows that
\Xi
is well-defined and bounded. Analogously one can check that
O
is well-defined and bounded. Hence, in order to show (i) it suffices to prove

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)Of=O(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)f=f

(4.11)

for all
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
.
By
Proposition
2.7
(iii) and
[
26
, Proposition 1.2.24]
this is true, if for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
the relation

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)Of(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})=O(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)f(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})=f(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})

holds a.e. on
(-1,1)
. Let
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
be fixed such that

\varphi=\varphi(x_{\Sigma}):=f(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})\in L^{2}((-1,1);\mathbb{C}^{N})
and set
A=A(x_{\Sigma}):=(\alpha\cdot\nu(x_{\Sigma}))V(x_{\Sigma})
. Then, we have for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)

\begin{split}&(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)Of(t)(x_{\Sigma})\\
&=\varphi(t)+\exp(-iAQ(t))\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})-iA\int_{-1}^{t}\exp(iA(Q(s)-Q(t)))q(s)\varphi(s)\,ds\\
&\,\,+\frac{i}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)Aq(s)\bigg(\varphi(s)+\exp(-iAQ(s))\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-iA\int_{-1}^{s}\exp(iA(Q(r)-Q(s)))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\bigg)\,ds.\end{split}

(4.12)

With a direct calculation we find that

\begin{split}\frac{i}{2}&\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)A\exp(-iAQ(s))q(s)\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})\,ds\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\int_{-1}^{t}iA\exp(-iAQ(s))q(s)\,ds-\int_{t}^{1}iA\exp(-iAQ(s))q(s)\,ds\bigg)\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})\\
&=-\exp(-iAQ(t))\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})+\frac{1}{2}\big(\exp\big(-i\tfrac{A}{2}\big)+\exp\big(i\tfrac{A}{2}\big)\big)\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})\\
&=-\exp(-iAQ(t))\Xi f(x_{\Sigma})+\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)\Xi f(x_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Furthermore, integration by parts gives us

\begin{split}-&\frac{i}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)Aq(s)iA\int_{-1}^{s}\exp(iA(Q(r)-Q(s)))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\,ds\\
&\quad=\frac{i}{2}A\int_{-1}^{t}\frac{d}{ds}\big(\exp(-iAQ(s))\big)\int_{-1}^{s}\exp(iAQ(r))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\,ds\\
&\qquad-\frac{i}{2}A\int_{t}^{1}\frac{d}{ds}\big(\exp(-iAQ(s))\big)\int_{-1}^{s}\exp(iAQ(r))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\,ds\\
&\quad=iA\exp(-iAQ(t))\int_{-1}^{t}\exp(iAQ(r))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\\
&\qquad-\frac{i}{2}A\int_{-1}^{1}\exp(iA(Q(r)-\tfrac{1}{2}))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr-\frac{i}{2}A\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)q(s)\varphi(s)\,ds\\
&\quad=iA\exp(-iAQ(t))\int_{-1}^{t}\exp(iAQ(r))q(r)\varphi(r)\,dr\\
&\qquad-\frac{i}{2}A\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)q(s)\varphi(s)\,ds-\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)\Xi f(x_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

A combination of the last two calculations with (
4.12
) yields

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)Of(t)(x_{\Sigma})=\varphi(t).

One verifies in a very similar way that
O
is also the left inverse of
I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq
. Consequently, (
4.11
) is true.

(ii) Let
f\in\textup{ran }\mathfrak{J}
, that is,
f
is independent of
t\in(-1,1)
. Instead of inserting
f
in (
4.10
)
we find it more convenient and easier to verify this claim directly by showing

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)f=f.

Similar as above it suffices to prove

(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)f(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})=f(\cdot)(x_{\Sigma})

for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma

a.e. on
(-1,1)
. Thus, we again fix
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and use the same abbreviations as in the proof of (i). Since here

f
is constant with respect to
t
also
\varphi=f(t)(x_{\Sigma})
is independent of
t
. We then compute

\begin{split}&(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})\\
&~=\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\!\exp(-iAQ(t))\varphi+\frac{i}{2}\!\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)Aq(s)\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ(s))\varphi ds\\
&~=\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ(t))\varphi-\frac{\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}}{2}\int_{-1}^{1}\textup{sign}(t-s)\frac{d}{ds}\exp(-iAQ(s))\,ds\varphi\\
&~=\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ(t))\varphi\\
&\qquad\qquad-\frac{\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}}{2}\Big(2\exp(-iAQ(t))-\exp(i\tfrac{A}{2})-\exp(-i\tfrac{A}{2})\Big)\varphi\\
&~=\varphi=f(t)(x_{\Sigma})\end{split}

for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
, which shows
(
4.8
)
.
∎

In the next lemma we study relations connecting the coefficient matrix
V
defined in (
1.6
) and the matrix
\widetilde{V}=VS
with

S=\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, assume that
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and set
\widetilde{V}=VS
, where
S
is as above; cf. (
1.9
).
Then, the following is true:

(i)

S,\widetilde{V}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and, in particular, the multiplication by
\widetilde{V}
gives rise to a bounded operator in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

(ii)

\mathfrak{J}^{*}q\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{J}=S
.

(iii)

(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}=\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})
.

(iv)

If (
3.29
) holds, then
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
and
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
are boundedly invertible in the spaces
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, respectively, and

(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}.

Proof.

(i) From
\alpha\cdot\nu,V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
we conclude
\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
using the power series of
sinc
. The assumption
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
implies
S\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and hence also
\widetilde{V}=VS\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Eventually, since the multiplication by any
B\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
gives rise to a bounded operator in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, the same is true for
\widetilde{V}
; cf. (
2.3
)

(ii) Recall that
\mathfrak{J}
is defined by (
2.7
) and that its adjoint acts as

\mathfrak{J}^{*}f=\int_{-1}^{1}f(t)\,dt,\qquad f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})).

As in the proof of the previous lemma we use the abbreviation
A=(\alpha\cdot\nu)V
. Then, with Proposition
2.7
(iii) we get for
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})

\begin{split}\mathfrak{J}^{*}q\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ)\mathfrak{J}\psi&=\int_{-1}^{1}\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ(s))q(s)(\mathfrak{J}\psi)(s)\,ds\\
&=\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\int_{-1/2}^{1/2}\exp(-iAr)\,dr\psi\\
&=\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\int_{0}^{1/2}2\cos(Ar)\,dr\psi.\end{split}

Using the power series of
sinc
one verifies
\int_{0}^{1/2}2\cos(Ar)\,dr=\textup{sinc}(\tfrac{A}{2})

and hence

\mathfrak{J}^{*}q\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-iAQ)\mathfrak{J}\psi=\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{A}{2}\big)^{-1}\psi\\
=S\psi

for
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. This shows (ii).

(iii) Using the definition of
\mathfrak{J}
and
\mathfrak{J}^{*}
, see (
2.7
), and the representation of
B_{0}(z)
in (
3.16
) one sees

B_{0}(z)=T(\alpha\cdot\nu)+\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}.

(4.13)

Hence, for
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
item (ii) above and Lemma
4.2
(ii) imply

\begin{split}&(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}\psi\\
&\hskip 70.0pt=\mathfrak{J}\psi+\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}Vq(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}\psi\\
&\hskip 70.0pt=\mathfrak{J}\psi+\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}Vq\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{J}\psi\\
&\hskip 70.0pt=\mathfrak{J}\psi+\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}VS\psi\\
&\hskip 70.0pt=\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\psi.\end{split}

(iv) First,
(
3.29
) and Remark
3.12
(ii) imply that
I+B_{0}(z)Vq
is boundedly invertible in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Moreover, as
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})

by assumption, the operator
I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq
is bijective in
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
according to Lemma
4.2
(i). Thus, it follows from (iii) and
\mathfrak{J}^{*}\mathfrak{J}=2I
that

\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{J}^{*}(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}

is the left inverse of
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
and, in particular,
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
is injective. To show that
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
is surjective consider
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then, there exists a unique
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
such that

(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}f=\mathfrak{J}\varphi.

(4.14)

Define

\psi:=\varphi-\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}Vq(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}f.

Since
V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
we conclude together with Proposition
2.9
(i) that
\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Using (
4.13
) and (
4.14
) we see that

\begin{split}\mathfrak{J}\psi&=\mathfrak{J}\varphi-\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}Vq(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}f\\
&=\big(I+B_{0}(z)Vq-\mathfrak{J}\mathcal{C}_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}Vq\big)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}f\\
&=f.\end{split}

Thus, we conclude with (iii) that

\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\psi=(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}\psi=\mathfrak{J}\varphi

and therefore also
(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})\psi=\varphi
. Hence, the operator
I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}
is surjective and
thus bijective. Finally, the formula for the inverse follows directly by applying
(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}
from the left and
(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}
from the right to the identity in (iii).
∎

In the next proposition we use the results from Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.3
to show that under the assumption in (
3.29
) the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) is self-adjoint and satisfies
(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}=\mathcal{L}(z)
, where
\mathcal{L}(z)
is given by (
4.1
).

Proposition 4.4
.

Let
z\in\rho(H)
,
q
and
V
be as in (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), respectively, assume that
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and set
\widetilde{V}=VS
, where
S
is given by (
1.9
).
If (
3.29
) holds, then the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) is self-adjoint in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
z\in\rho(H_{\widetilde{V}})
, and one has

(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}=R_{z}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)=\mathcal{L}(z).

Proof.

The proof of this proposition consists of two steps. In
Step 1
we show that for the given
z\in\rho(H)
satisfying (
3.29
) the operator

\mathcal{L}(z)=R_{z}-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)

fulfils
\mathcal{L}(z)=(H_{0}-z)^{-1}
for a self-adjoint operator
H_{0}
, and in
Step 2
we prove
H_{0}=H_{\widetilde{V}}
.

Step 1.
First, we check that
\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Recall that
H
is defined by (
2.8
), let
u\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})=\textup{dom }H
and set
v:=(H-z)u
, which is equivalent to
u=R_{z}v
. Then, it follows with (
3.13
) and Proposition
2.8
that

C_{0}(z)v=\mathfrak{J}\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}(R_{z}v)=\mathfrak{J}\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}u=0

and hence,

u=R_{z}v-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)v=\mathcal{L}(z)v.

We conclude that
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
and therefore
\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
is dense.

To show that
\mathcal{L}(z)
is the resolvent of a self-adjoint operator, we first consider the case
z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\mathbb{R}\subset\rho(H)
such that (
3.29
) holds. Note that, by Proposition
4.1
,
\mathcal{L}(z)
is the limit in the operator norm of
(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}
as
\varepsilon\to 0+
, which are resolvents of self-adjoint operators. Moreover,
(H_{\varepsilon}-\overline{z})^{-1}
converges to
\mathcal{L}(z)^{*}
as
\varepsilon\to 0+
. Thus, as
\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, we conclude from
[
37
, Theorem VIII.22]
that there exists a self-adjoint operator
H_{0}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

z\in\rho(H_{0})
and
\mathcal{L}(z)=(H_{0}-z)^{-1}
.

Eventually, consider
z\in\mathbb{R}\cap\rho(H)
such that (
3.29
) holds. Then,
\mathcal{L}(z)
is self-adjoint, as it is the limit of the bounded and self-adjoint operators
(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}
. Moreover, since
\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
is dense in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, we have

\ker\mathcal{L}(z)=(\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z))^{\perp}=\{0\}
, which shows
\mathcal{L}(z)
is injective. Hence,

H_{0}:=z+(\mathcal{L}(z))^{-1}

defines a self-adjoint operator with
z\in\rho(H_{0})
and
(H_{0}-z)^{-1}=\mathcal{L}(z)
.

Step 2.
We show that
H_{\widetilde{V}}=H_{0}
. Since
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}
, the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is densely defined. Moreover, as
\widetilde{V}=\widetilde{V}^{*}

we compute for
u,v\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}
using
integration by parts (see (
C.1
))

\begin{split}(H_{\widetilde{V}}u,v)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}&-(u,H_{\widetilde{V}}v)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-},i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}v_{+}-\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}v_{-})\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\qquad-\frac{1}{2}\big(i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}-\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-}),\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}v_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}v_{-}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=-\frac{1}{4}\big(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-},\widetilde{V}(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}v_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}v_{-})\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{4}\big(\widetilde{V}(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-}),\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}v_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}v_{-}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=0,\end{split}

where the jump condition for
u,v\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) was used in the last step. Therefore,
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is symmetric and to see
H_{\widetilde{V}}=H_{0}
it suffices to prove
H_{0}\subset H_{\widetilde{V}}
.
Let
z\in\rho(H)
such that
(
3.29
) holds and
let
u\in\textup{dom }H_{0}=\textup{ran }\mathcal{L}(z)
.
Then, there exists
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
such that

u=(H_{0}-z)^{-1}v=\mathcal{L}(z)v=R_{z}v-A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)v.

(4.15)

We show that
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})=H^{1}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{1}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. By (
3.13
), Lemma
4.3
(iv), and (
4.8
) we get

\begin{split}&(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)v\\
&\qquad\qquad=(I+T(\alpha\cdot\nu)Vq)^{-1}\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\\
&\qquad\qquad=\cos\big((\alpha\cdot\nu)\tfrac{V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v.\end{split}

With
A_{0}(z)=\Phi_{z}\mathfrak{J}^{*}
, Lemma
4.3
(ii), and
VS=\widetilde{V}
we conclude from this

\begin{split}A_{0}(z)&Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)v\\
&=\Phi_{z}V\mathfrak{J}^{*}q\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\exp(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)VQ)\mathfrak{J}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\\
&=\Phi_{z}VS(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\\
&=\Phi_{z}\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v.\end{split}

(4.16)

It follows from Proposition
2.8
and Lemma
4.3
(i), (iv) that

\Phi_{z}\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and since

R_{z}v\in\textup{dom }H=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
we conclude from (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) that
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Next, we show that
u
satisfies the transmission condition in
\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}
.
Note that
R_{z}v\in\textup{dom }H=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
. This, (
4.16
), (
2.13
), (
2.14
), and Proposition
2.9
(ii) (applied to
\varphi=\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}(\Phi_{\overline{z}})^{*}v)
yield

\begin{split}\frac{\widetilde{V}}{2}&(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-})+i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}-\mathbf{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-})\\
&=\widetilde{V}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v-\widetilde{V}\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v-i(\alpha\cdot\nu)(-i(\alpha\cdot\nu))\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\\
&=\widetilde{V}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v-\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\\
&=0.\end{split}

Hence,
u\in\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}
.
Finally, we get with
(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})R_{z}v=v
and Proposition
2.8
(ii) that

\begin{split}[(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)u]_{\pm}&=(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})u_{\pm}\\
&=(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})(R_{z}v)_{\pm}\\
&\qquad-(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta-zI_{N})\big(\Phi_{z}\widetilde{V}(I+\mathcal{C}_{z}\widetilde{V})^{-1}\Phi_{\overline{z}}^{*}v\big)_{\pm}\\
&=v_{\pm}\\
&=[(H_{0}-z)u]_{\pm}.\end{split}

Therefore,
H_{0}\subset H_{\widetilde{V}}
and the proof is complete.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Combining the results from Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.4
we find that
H_{\widetilde{V}}
is self-adjoint and for
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
we have

\begin{split}\|(H_{\varepsilon}&-z)^{-1}-(H_{\widetilde{V}}-z)^{-1}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=\big\|(H_{\varepsilon}-z)^{-1}-R_{z}+A_{0}(z)Vq(I+B_{0}(z)Vq)^{-1}C_{0}(z)\big\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-r}.\end{split}

Hence, Theorem
1.1
is proved
∎

Now we prove Corollary
1.2
, where the special case
V=\eta I_{N}+\tau\beta+\lambda i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\beta
with real-valued functions
\eta,\tau,\lambda\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{R})
is considered.

Proof of Corollary
1.2
.

Let
d=\eta^{2}-\tau^{2}-\lambda^{2}
. Then
((\alpha\cdot\nu)V)^{2}=dI_{N}
and thus,

\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)V/2)^{2j}}{(2j)!}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{(\sqrt{d}/2)^{2j}}{(2j)!}I_{N}=\cos\big(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\big)I_{N}

and

\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)V/2)^{2j}}{(2j+1)!}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}\frac{(\sqrt{d}/2)^{2j}}{(2j+1)!}I_{N}=\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{\sqrt{d}}{2}\big)I_{N}.

Hence, the condition
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
in (
1.11
) reduces to

\inf_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\left\lvert{(2k+1)^{2}\pi^{2}-d(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert>0

and
S=\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}=2\tfrac{\tan(\sqrt{d}/2)}{\sqrt{d}}I_{N}
.
Therefore, Corollary
1.2
follows immediately from Theorem
1.1
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.3
.

First, we mention some identities for functions of matrices that will be useful in the proof. For
A\in\mathbb{C}^{N\times N}
with
\left\lvert{A}\right\rvert<1
the series

\arctan(A)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{A^{2n+1}}{2n+1}

converges absolutely with respect to the matrix norm (in our case the Frobenius norm), and with the help of (
1.17
) (see also
[
19
, Chapter VII. §4]
and
[
20
, Chapter VIII.3.1]
) one finds that
\cos(\arctan(A))
is invertible and

\begin{split}\sin(\arctan(A))\cos(\arctan(A))^{-1}&=A,\\
\cos(\arctan(A))^{-1}&=\cos(\arctan(A))(I_{N}+A^{2}).\end{split}

(4.17)

Now, set

V=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big).

It suffices to show that
V
fulfils the assumptions of Theorem
1.1
, i.e. (
1.10
) and (
1.11
), and
\widetilde{V}=SV
, as then the claim follows from Theorem
1.1
. The assumption (
1.14
) implies
\|(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}\|_{W_{\infty}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}<2
. Therefore, the power series defining
\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)
converges in
W_{\infty}^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
and hence also

V\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Pointwise application of the results mentioned above and
\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}=\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)
show
that
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)=\cos\big(\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)\big)
is invertible

\sigma
-a.e. and (
4.17
) yields

\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}=\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\Big(I_{N}+\tfrac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V})^{2}}{4}\Big)\quad\sigma\textup{-a.e. on }\Sigma.

As
\alpha\cdot\nu,\widetilde{V},V,\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
, this implies that also
\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\in W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})
. Similarly, we get with (
4.17
)

\begin{split}VS&=V\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\\
&=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\frac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\textup{sinc}\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)\cos\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)V}{2}\big)^{-1}\\
&=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\sin\big(\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)\big)\cos\big(\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}}{2}\big)\big)^{-1}\\
&=\widetilde{V}.\end{split}

Finally, we verify that (
1.10
) is satisfied. By (
1.14
) and the definition of
V
we have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}&\leq{\left\lVert{\alpha\cdot\nu}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\left\lVert{\frac{((\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V})^{2n+1}}{2^{2n+1}(2n+1)}}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}\\
&={\left\lVert{\alpha\cdot\nu}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}\textup{artanh}\Big(\tfrac{\|(\alpha\cdot\nu)\widetilde{V}\|_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}}{2}\Big)\\
&<X_{z}.\end{split}

Hence, (
1.10
) is fulfilled with
q=\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{(-1,1)}
. This finishes the proof of Corollary
1.3
.
∎

In the following remark we comment on the condition
{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}<X_{z}
in (
1.10
), which is the main restriction in Theorem
1.1
, and explain that it is sharp (in a certain sense).

Remark 4.5
.

Consider the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}
in (
1.2
) for a so-called critical interaction strength in
the purely electrostatic setting with
m>0
and
\Sigma
compact and smooth, that is, we set

\widetilde{V}=\pm 2I_{N}
. In this case it is known that the operator
H_{\widetilde{V}}

defined on functions from
H^{1}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{1}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N})
that satisfy the transmission conditions in
\textup{dom }H_{\widetilde{V}}

is essentially self-adjoint, but not self-adjoint, and one has
\sigma_{\textup{ess}}(\overline{H_{\widetilde{V}}})\cap(-m,m)\neq\emptyset
; cf.
[
10
,
14
]
and the references therein for more details.
In particular,
H_{\widetilde{V}}
or
\overline{H_{\widetilde{V}}}
can not be the norm resolvent limit of self-adjoint operators
H_{\varepsilon}
, as
\sigma_{\textup{ess}}(H_{\varepsilon})=(-\infty,-m]\cup[m,\infty)
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2}]
, see, e.g.,
[
41
, Theorem 4.7 and (4.53)]
for the case
\theta=3
.
However, to construct
approximating operators with potentials
V_{\varepsilon}

Corollary
1.3
would suggest the specific choices

q=\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{(-1,1)}
and

V=2(\alpha\cdot\nu)\arctan\big(\tfrac{(\alpha\cdot\nu)(\pm 2I_{N})}{2}\big)=\arctan I_{N}=\frac{\pi}{2}I_{N},

and in this situation

{\left\lVert{V}\right\rVert}_{W^{1}_{\infty}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N\times N})}{\left\lVert{q}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R})}=\frac{\pi}{4},

which is exactly the upper bound for
X_{z}
from Lemma
3.13
(ii).
Hence, this critical case can not be treated by Theorem
1.1
and the upper bound for
X_{z}
in Lemma
3.13
(ii) is sharp in the sense that it does not allow electrostatic interactions being larger (in absolute value) or equal to the critical values
\pm 2
.

Appendix A
Properties of the map
\iota

In this section we investigate the map

\iota:\Sigma\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\theta},\qquad\iota(x_{\Sigma},t):=x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma}),

(A.1)

defined in (
1.3
). The goal is to show that for some
\varepsilon_{A}>0
the mapping

\iota
and their local counterparts
\iota_{l}
defined below in (
A.2
) are bi-Lipschitz on
\Sigma\times(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
and
\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
, respectively, provided that
\varepsilon_{A}
is sufficiently small.
Recall that
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(x^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}))
for
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
and
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, where
\kappa_{l}
is a rotation matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times\theta}
and
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
; cf. Hypothesis
2.1
and (
2.1
).
Besides the map
\iota
in (
A.1
) we shall also make use of the maps

\iota_{l}:\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{\theta},\qquad\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t):=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})+t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime}),

(A.2)

where
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and the unit normal vector field
\nu_{l}
on
\Sigma_{l}
is given by

\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\kappa_{l}(-\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}),1)}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}},\qquad x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}.

Note that if
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
is such that
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, then
\nu(x_{\Sigma})=\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})
and
\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)=\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

First, we provide a variant of the mean value theorem for vector- and matrix-valued functions which will be used frequently in the following.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
k,l,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set, and
A\in C^{1}_{b}(U;\mathbb{C}^{k\times l})
. If
x,y\in U
and the line segment connecting
x
and
y
is contained in
U
, then

\begin{split}\left\lvert{A(x)-A(y)}\right\rvert&\leq\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\left\lvert{x-y}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\sqrt{n}\sup_{\mu\in[0,1],j\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert\left\lvert{x-y}\right\rvert.\end{split}

In particular, if
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
l=1
, then

\left\lvert{A(x)-A(y)}\right\rvert\leq{\left\lVert{DA}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{k\times n})}\left\lvert{x-y}\right\rvert,\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Proof.

Recall that
|\cdot|
denotes, depending on the argument, the absolute value, the Euclidean vector norm, or the Frobenius matrix norm.
The fundamental theorem of calculus and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality lead to

\begin{split}\left\lvert{A(x)-A(y)}\right\rvert&=\bigg|\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))\,(x-y)_{j}\,d\mu\bigg|\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert\left\lvert{(x-y)_{j}}\right\rvert\,d\mu\\
&\leq\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert\left\lvert{(x-y)_{j}}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\left\lvert{x-y}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\sqrt{n}\sup_{\mu\in[0,1],j\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}A)(x+\mu(y-x))}\right\rvert\left\lvert{x-y}\right\rvert.\end{split}

The estimate for the special case
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
l=1
is an immediate consequence of the above estimate.
∎

Now we turn to the properties of the mappings
\iota
and
\iota_{l}
in (
A.1
) and
(
A.2
), respectively.

Proposition A.2
.

Let
\Omega_{\pm},\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
, and let
\iota
and
\iota_{l}
,

l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, be as in (
A.1
) and (
A.2
). Then there exists
\varepsilon_{A}>0
and constants
C_{A,1},C_{A,2}>0
such that the following holds:

(i)

For all
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
and
t,s\in(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
we have for all
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}

C_{A,1}^{-1}\bigl(\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr)\leq\left\lvert{\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)-\iota_{l}(y^{\prime},s)}\right\rvert\leq C_{A,1}\bigl(\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr).

(ii)

For all
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
t,s\in(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
we have

C_{A,2}^{-1}\bigl(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr)\leq\left\lvert{\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)-\iota(y_{\Sigma},s)}\right\rvert\leq C_{A,2}\bigl(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr).

Proof.

(i) Let
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
and
t,s\in(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
be fixed, where
\varepsilon_{A}>0
is, at the moment, a fixed number. Using (
A.2
),
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(x^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})),x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(y^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime}))
,
and Lemma
A.1
we find

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)-\iota_{l}(y^{\prime},s)}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\varepsilon_{A}\left\lvert{\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+{\left\lVert{\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})}\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+\varepsilon_{A}{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq\big(1+{\left\lVert{\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})}+\varepsilon_{A}{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}\big)\bigl(\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr).\end{split}

Now the second estimate in (i) follows if we
fix
0<\varepsilon_{A}\leq 1
and choose

C_{A,1}\geq 1+\max_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}\big({\left\lVert{\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})}+{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}\big),

which is finite since we assumed in Hypothesis
2.1
that
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
for all
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.

Next, we prove the first inequality in (i). We start by rewriting

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)-\iota_{l}(y^{\prime},s)}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\hskip 40.0pt+2{\left\langle{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})},{t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rangle}+\left\lvert{t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}.\end{split}

(A.3)

We are going to estimate all three terms on the right hand side separately. For the first one, we find with
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(x^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}))
,
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})=\kappa_{l}(y^{\prime},\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime}))
, and as
\kappa_{l}
is unitary that

\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}=|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}+|\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})|^{2}\geq|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}.

(A.4)

Next, we consider the second term on the right hand side of (
A.3
). We start by observing

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}&:=\big|\langle x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime}),t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})\rangle\big|\\
&=\Bigg|\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}{\left\langle{\kappa_{l}\begin{pmatrix}x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}\\
\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})\end{pmatrix}},{t\kappa_{l}\begin{pmatrix}-\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})\\
1\end{pmatrix}}\right\rangle}\Bigg|\\
&=\Bigg|t\frac{{\left\langle{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}},{-\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rangle}+\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}\Bigg|.\end{split}

The mean value theorem shows
\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})={\left\langle{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}},{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}+\mu(y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}))}\right\rangle}
for some
\mu\in[0,1]
.
Using Lemma
A.1
the above expression can be further estimated by

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}&\leq\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}\Bigg|t\frac{{\left\langle{y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}},{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime}+\mu(y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}))}\right\rangle}}{\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}}\Bigg|\\
&\leq\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}\varepsilon_{A}\left\lvert{y^{\prime}-x^{\prime}}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\mu(y^{\prime}-x^{\prime})}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{(\theta-1)\times(\theta-1)})}\\
&\leq\varepsilon_{A}\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert^{2}{\left\lVert{D\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{(\theta-1)\times(\theta-1)})}.\end{split}

Similarly, one has

\begin{split}\left|{\left\langle{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})},{s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rangle}\right|&\leq\varepsilon_{A}\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert^{2}{\left\lVert{D\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{(\theta-1)\times(\theta-1)})},\end{split}

and thus

\begin{split}&2{\left\langle{x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})},{t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rangle}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq-4\varepsilon_{A}\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert^{2}{\left\lVert{D\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{(\theta-1)\times(\theta-1)})}.\end{split}

(A.5)

Eventually, to estimate the third term on the right hand side in (
A.3
), we use Lemma
A.1
as well as
(a-b)^{2}\geq\tfrac{1}{2}a^{2}-b^{2}
for
a,b>0
and calculate

\begin{split}\left\lvert{t\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}&=\left\lvert{(t-s)\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})-s(\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})-\nu_{l}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\geq\big(\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert-\left\lvert{s(\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})-\nu_{l}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert\big)^{2}\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{2}-s^{2}\left\lvert{\nu_{l}(y^{\prime})-\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{2}-\varepsilon_{A}^{2}|x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}|^{2}{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}^{2}.\end{split}

(A.6)

By combining (
A.4
)–(
A.6
) in (
A.3
) we obtain

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)-\iota_{l}(y^{\prime},s)}\right\rvert^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\quad+\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert^{2}\Big(1-4\varepsilon_{A}{\left\lVert{D\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{(\theta-1)\times(\theta-1)})}-\varepsilon_{A}^{2}{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}^{2}\Big).\end{split}

As before we conclude from
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
that for
\varepsilon_{A}>0
sufficiently small and
C_{A,1}>0
sufficiently large also the first inequality in (i) is fulfilled.

(ii) We fix
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
t,s\in(-\varepsilon_{A},\varepsilon_{A})
. Let us first assume that
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma_{l}
for some
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. Then there exist
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
such that
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})
and
y_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(y^{\prime})
, and therefore
\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)=\iota_{l}(x^{\prime},t)
and
