\iota(y_{\Sigma},s)=\iota_{l}(y^{\prime},s)
. In this case we see

\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert=\sqrt{\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert{\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})-\zeta_{l}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}

and therefore combining

\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert{x^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert\sqrt{1+{\left\lVert{\nabla\zeta_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1})}^{2}}

with (i) yields (ii). It remains to consider the case where
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and there is no
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma_{l}
. Then, (ii) and (iii) from Hypothesis
2.1
imply
\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\geq\varepsilon_{0}
, where
\varepsilon_{0}
is the number specified in Hypothesis
2.1
. Choose
\varepsilon_{A}\leq\varepsilon_{0}/6
. Then,
\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\geq 6\varepsilon_{A}
,
\left\lvert{t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-s\nu(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\leq 2\varepsilon_{A}
, and
\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\leq 2\varepsilon_{A}
yield

\left\lvert{\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)-\iota(y_{\Sigma},s)}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+2\varepsilon_{A}\leq\frac{4}{3}\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\leq\frac{4}{3}(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)

and

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert\bigr)&\leq\frac{\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert}{2}+\varepsilon_{A}=\frac{\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert}{2}+3\varepsilon_{A}-2\varepsilon_{A}\\
&\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert-2\varepsilon_{A}\leq\left\lvert{\iota(x_{\Sigma},t)-\iota(y_{\Sigma},s)}\right\rvert,\end{split}

which imply (ii) also in this case.
∎

Eventually, we state a useful consequence of Proposition
A.2
.

Corollary A.3
.

Assume that
\Sigma,\Omega_{\pm}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
and let
\varepsilon_{A}
be as in Proposition
A.2
. Then, the following holds:

(i)

For any
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
t\in(0,\varepsilon_{A})
one has
x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Omega_{-}
.

(ii)

For any
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
t\in(-\varepsilon_{A},0)
one has
x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Omega_{+}
.

Proof.

We show item (i), the proof of assertion (ii) follows the same lines. The claim will be verified by an indirect proof. Assume that there are
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
t\in(0,\varepsilon_{A})
such that
x_{\Sigma}+t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\notin\Omega_{-}
. Since
\nu
is pointing outwards of
\Omega_{+}
, we have for small
\mu>0
that
x_{\Sigma}+\mu t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Omega_{-}
. By continuity, this implies that there exists
\mu_{0}\in(0,1]
such that
x_{\Sigma}+\mu_{0}t\nu(x_{\Sigma})\in\Sigma
. However, we obtain from Proposition
A.2
for all
y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
with a constant
C_{A,2}>0
the inequality

\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}+\mu_{0}t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\geq C_{A,2}^{-1}\mu_{0}t>0;

this is a contradiction.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of the estimate (
3.25
)

Let
z\in\rho(H)
and
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
be the operators defined by (
3.17
) and (
3.19
), respectively. In this appendix we show that
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
can be extended to a bounded operator from
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and that

{\left\lVert{\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{0\to 1/2}\leq C(\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert)^{1/2}

(B.1)

for some
C>0
, which is used in (
3.25
) in
Step 3
in the proof of Proposition
3.10
.
With (
3.18
) and (
3.23
) one obtains for
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))

\begin{split}\bigl(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)\bigr)f(t)(x_{\Sigma})=&\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{\Sigma}\big(G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\\
&-G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma}))\big)f(s)(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,ds\end{split}

(B.2)

for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
and for
\sigma
-a.e.
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, where
G_{z}
is the integral kernel of
R_{z}=(H-z)^{-1}
;
cf. (
2.10
)–(
2.11
). Thus, in order to show (
B.1
), we proceed as follows: We prove in Proposition
B.3
that for fixed
t\neq s\in(-1,1)
the operator formally acting on
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
as

\begin{split}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma})=&\int_{\Sigma}\big(G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\\
&\quad-G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma}))\big)\psi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}),\end{split}

(B.3)

x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, gives rise to a bounded operator from
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and we prove a norm estimate for its operator norm. Then we show in Lemma
B.4
that the map
(s,t)\mapsto b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)
is measurable and use (
B.2
) to transfer the results from
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)
to
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
.

In the following, we always assume
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
with
\varepsilon_{2}>0
satisfying (
3.11
). Recall that
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon_{A}
are the numbers that are specified in Propositions
2.4
and
A.2
, respectively. Since
\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{A}
, see the proof of Proposition
2.4
, we conclude from (
3.11
) that
\varepsilon_{2}<\tfrac{\varepsilon_{A}}{2}
. Define for
t\neq s\in(-1,1)
and
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma

\begin{split}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)&:=G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma}))\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad-G_{z}(x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})).\end{split}

(B.4)

Moreover, we introduce for
t\neq s\in(-1,1)
and
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
the quantities

\begin{split}z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)&:={x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma})}=\iota(x_{\Sigma},\varepsilon t)-\iota(y_{\Sigma},\varepsilon s),\\
z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)&:={x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})}=\iota(x_{\Sigma},\varepsilon(t-s))-\iota(y_{\Sigma},0),\\
z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)&:=\mu z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)+(1-\mu)z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)\\
&=\iota(x_{\Sigma},(\mu\varepsilon t+(1-\mu)\varepsilon(t-s))-\iota(y_{\Sigma},\mu\varepsilon s)\quad\text{for }\mu\in(0,1),\\
L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)&:=\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\varepsilon(t-s)}\right\rvert.\\
\end{split}

Then,
\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)=G_{z}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s))-G_{z}(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s))
.
It follows from Proposition
A.2
(ii) that for
\mu\in[0,1]

C_{A,2}^{-1}L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)\leq\left\lvert{z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)}\right\rvert\leq C_{A,2}L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)

(B.5)

holds. To shorten notation we also set

\omega:=\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}C_{A,2}^{-1}>0,\quad z\in\rho(H).

(B.6)

Furthermore, until Lemma
B.4
we fix
t\neq s\in(-1,1)
and hence omit the arguments
t,s
in the functions
L
,
\Delta G_{z}
,
z_{0}
,
z_{\mu}
and
z_{1}
.

Lemma B.1
.

Let
G_{z}
be the integral kernel of
R_{z}
in (
2.10
)–(
2.11
),
\Delta G_{z}
as in (
B.4
),
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, and
x_{\Sigma_{l}}
as in (
2.1
). Then the following is true:

(i)

There exists
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
s
such that

\left\lvert{\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}

for all
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
.

(ii)

There exists
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
s
such that

\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon\left(L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}}\right)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}

for all
k\in\{1,\dots,\theta-1\}
,
y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, and
x^{\prime}\in x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)
.

Proof.

Before we prove (i) and (ii) we show useful estimates of the difference
z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})-z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})
and
\partial_{j}G_{z}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}
.
Since
\varepsilon_{2}<\tfrac{\varepsilon_{A}}{2}
, it follows from Proposition
A.2
(ii) that

\begin{split}\big|z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})-z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\big|&=\big|\varepsilon t\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon s\nu(y_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon(t-s)\nu(x_{\Sigma})\big|\\
&=\varepsilon|s|\left\lvert{\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\nu(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{2}}\left\lvert{\varepsilon_{2}\nu(x_{\Sigma})-\varepsilon_{2}\nu(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{2}}\big(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\big|x_{\Sigma}+\varepsilon_{2}\nu(x_{\Sigma})-y_{\Sigma}-\varepsilon_{2}\nu(y_{\Sigma})\big|\big)\\
&=\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon_{2}}\big(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{\iota(x_{\Sigma},\varepsilon_{2})-\iota(y_{\Sigma},\varepsilon_{2})}\right\rvert\big)\\
&\leq\frac{1+C_{A,2}}{\varepsilon_{2}}\varepsilon\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{1+C_{A,2}}{\varepsilon_{2}}\varepsilon L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\end{split}

(B.7)

for all
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
. Next, we estimate
\partial_{j}G_{z}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}
. For
\partial_{j}G_{z}

we obtain for
\theta=2
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
from (
2.10
)

\begin{split}\partial_{j}G_{z}(x)=&\frac{\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}}{2\pi}K_{1}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}-\frac{x_{j}(\alpha\cdot x)}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{3}}\bigg)\\
&+i\frac{z^{2}-m^{2}}{4\pi}\Big(K_{0}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)+K_{2}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\Big)\frac{x_{j}(\alpha\cdot x)}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{2}}\\
&+\frac{i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}}{2\pi}K_{1}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\frac{x_{j}}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}\left(m\beta+zI_{2}\right),\end{split}

where we used
K_{0}^{\prime}=-K_{1}
and
K_{1}^{\prime}=-\tfrac{1}{2}(K_{0}+K_{2})
; cf.
[
33
, §10.29(i)]
.
For
\theta=3
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus\{0\}
we obtain from (
2.11
)

\begin{split}\partial_{j}G_{z}(x)=\Bigg(i\Big(1-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\Big)\bigg(\alpha_{j}-\frac{2x_{j}(\alpha\cdot x)}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)+\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\frac{x_{j}(\alpha\cdot x)}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}&\\
+\bigg(zI_{4}+m\beta+i\Big(1-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\Big)\frac{\alpha\cdot x}{\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{2}}\bigg)\Big(i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}x_{j}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert-x_{j}\Big)&\Bigg)\\
\cdot\frac{e^{i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}}{4\pi\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{3}}&.\end{split}

By the well-known asymptotic expansions of the modified Bessel functions from
[
33
, §10.25 (ii) & §10.30 (i)]
, there exists
R>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\{0\}
with
|x|\leq R
one has

\big|K_{n}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\big|\leq C\begin{cases}\left\lvert{\log\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}\right\rvert,&n=0,\\
\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{-n},&n\in\{1,2\},\end{cases}

and for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\{0\}
with
|x|>R
and
n\in\{0,1,2\}

\big|K_{n}\big(-i\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert\big)\big|\leq Ce^{-\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert}.

Using this in the above formulas for
\partial_{j}G_{z}
, one concludes that

\begin{split}\sup_{j\in\{1,\dots\,,\theta\}}\left\lvert{\partial_{j}G_{z}(x)}\right\rvert\leq C\big(1+\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{-\theta}\big)e^{-\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\{0\}.\end{split}

(B.8)

(i) Applying Lemma
A.1
, (
B.7
), (
B.8
), and (
B.5
) yields

\begin{split}\left\lvert{\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert&=\left\lvert{G_{z}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}))-G_{z}(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert\\
&\leq C\sup_{\mu\in[0,1],j\in\{1,\dots\,,\theta\}}\left\lvert{\partial_{j}G_{z}(z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert{z_{0}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})-z_{1}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\\
&\leq C\sup_{\mu\in[0,1],j\in\{1,\dots\,,\theta\}}\left\lvert{\partial_{j}G_{z}(z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert\varepsilon L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\sup_{\mu\in[0,1]}(1+\left\lvert{z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{-\theta})e^{-\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert}\varepsilon L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\varepsilon(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\end{split}

for all
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
,
where
\omega
is defined in (
B.6
) and
C>0
is a constant which does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
s
. Hence, the claim in (i) is shown.

(ii) For
k\in\{1,\dots,\theta-1\}
,
y_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
, and
x^{\prime}\in x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)
we compute

\begin{split}&\hskip-15.0pt\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})=\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\big(G_{z}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))-G_{z}(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\big)\\
=&\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))_{j}\\
&-\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))_{j}\\
=&\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\big((\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))-(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\big)\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))_{j}\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\left(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})-z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})\right)_{j}\\
=&\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\big((\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))-(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\big)\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))_{j}\\
&+\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\varepsilon s(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\left(\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})\right)_{j},\end{split}

where
\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})=\nu(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))
was used in the last step.
To estimate the second sum we use (
B.5
), (
B.8
),
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
and
1+a^{-\theta}\leq 2(a+a^{-\theta})
for
a>0
and obtain

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\varepsilon s(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\left(\nu_{l}(x^{\prime})\right)_{j}}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 80.0pt\leq C\varepsilon\sup_{j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}}\left\lvert{(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{D\nu_{l}}\right\rVert}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R}^{\theta\times(\theta-1)})}\\
&\hskip 80.0pt\leq C\varepsilon(1+L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})^{-\theta})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\\
&\hskip 80.0pt\leq C\varepsilon(L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})^{-\theta})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}.\end{split}

For the remaining part given by

\sum_{j=1}^{\theta}\big((\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))-(\partial_{j}G_{z})(z_{1}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))\big)\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(z_{0}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))_{j}

(B.9)

we proceed in the same way as in the proof of (i). Using Lemma
A.1
and
\zeta_{l}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
one can show that the absolute value of the expression in (
B.9
) is bounded by the term

C\sup_{\mu\in[0,1],\,n,j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}}\left\lvert{\partial_{n}\partial_{j}G_{z}(z_{\mu}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}))}\right\rvert\varepsilon L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma}).

Moreover, in a similar way as in (
B.8
) one can prove

\begin{split}\sup_{n,j\in\{1,\dots,\theta\}}\left\lvert{\partial_{n}\partial_{j}G_{z}(x)}\right\rvert\leq C(1+\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert^{-\theta-1})e^{-\textup{Im}\sqrt{z^{2}-m^{2}}\left\lvert{x}\right\rvert},\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\{0\}.\end{split}

Combining these observations with (
B.5
) yield the claim in (ii).
∎

To estimate the operator norm of
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)
in Proposition
B.3
below we will make use of a partition of unity
for
\Sigma
subordinate to
(W_{l})_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
with the additional property that the derivatives are uniformly bounded; the existence of
such a partition of unity (in the case that
\Sigma
is unbounded) is shown in the next lemma.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
\theta\in\{2,3\}
, satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
. Then, there exists a partition of unity
(\varphi_{l})_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
subordinate to the open cover
(W_{l})_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
of
\Sigma
such that
\varphi_{l}\in C^{\infty}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{R})
for all
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.

Proof.

According to
[
39
, Appendix A, Lemmas 1.2 and 1.3]
there exists a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<\delta<\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}
, and a sequence of real-valued
C^{\infty}
-functions
(\phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
(B(x_{n},\delta))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an open cover of
\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
,
(\phi_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a partition of unity subordinate
to this open cover,
\textup{supp }\phi_{n}\subset B(x_{n},\delta)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, every point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
is contained in at most
M
of the sets
B(x_{n},\delta)
, and the derivatives of the functions
\phi_{n}
are uniformly bounded. Next, we define the set
Y:=\{x_{n}:B(x_{n},\delta)\cap\Sigma\neq\emptyset\}
. Note that for all
x_{n}\in Y
there exists
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
such that
B(x_{n},\delta)\subset W_{l}
. In fact, as
B(x_{n},\delta)\cap\Sigma\neq\emptyset
there exists
y_{\Sigma}\in B(x_{n},\delta)\cap\Sigma
and thus, item (ii) in Hypothesis
2.1
implies
B(y_{\Sigma},\varepsilon_{0})\subset W_{l}
for an
l\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. Hence, for any
y\in B(x_{n},\delta)
one has

\left\lvert{y-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert{y-x_{n}}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{x_{n}-y_{\Sigma}}\right\rvert<2\delta<\varepsilon_{0},

which shows
B(x_{n},\delta)\subset W_{l}
. Define the sets
I_{1}:=\{n:x_{n}\in Y,B(x_{n},\delta)\subset W_{1}\}
and
I_{l}:=\{n:x_{n}\in Y,B(x_{n},\delta)\subset W_{l},B(x_{n},\delta)\not\subset W_{k},k\in\{1,\dots,l-1\}\}
for
l\in\{2,\dots,p\}
. Then, it is not difficult to see that

\varphi_{l}=\sum_{n\in I_{l}}\phi_{n}

is a partition of unity having the claimed properties.
∎

Proposition B.3
.

Let
t\neq s\in(-1,1)
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
. Then the operator formally defined by (
B.3
)
gives rise to a bounded operator
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z):L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and there
exists
C>0
which does not depend on
\varepsilon
,
t
, and
s
such that

{\left\lVert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}.

(B.10)

Proof.

We split this proof into four steps. In
Step 1
we verify the preliminary estimate

\begin{split}\sup_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}\Big(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L&(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{j}\Big)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\begin{cases}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert,&j=1-\theta,\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert},&j=-\theta,\end{cases}\end{split}

(B.11)

which will be used in
Step 2
and
Step 3
to obtain bounds for
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)

viewed as an operator from
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and from
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, respectively. Finally, we conclude with an interpolation argument
(
B.10
) in
Step 4
.

Step 1.

Let
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
and
j\in\{1-\theta,-\theta\}
. Recall that
\Sigma
satisfies Hypothesis
2.1
and let
(\varphi_{l})_{l\in\{1,\dots,p\}}
be the partition of unity from Lemma
B.2
. Using the definition of the boundary integral, we can write

\begin{split}\int_{\Sigma}\big(L(x_{\Sigma}&,y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{j}\big)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&=\sum_{n=1}^{p}\int_{x_{\Sigma_{n}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}(L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))+L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))^{j})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\varphi_{n}(x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{n}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}\,dy^{\prime}.\end{split}

Hence,
0\leq\varphi_{n}\leq 1
,
\zeta_{n}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1};\mathbb{R})
, and
x_{\Sigma_{n}}^{-1}(\Sigma)\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
yield

\begin{split}\int_{\Sigma}&(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{j})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\max_{n\in\{1,\dots,p\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}(L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))+L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))^{j})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))}\,dy^{\prime}\\
&\leq C\max_{n\in\{1,\dots,p\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}(\omega L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))+\omega^{j}L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))^{j})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))}\,dy^{\prime},\\
&\end{split}

where
C>0
does not depend on
x_{\Sigma}
,
t
,
s
, and
\varepsilon
.
Next, let
n\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and fix
x_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
such that
\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(x_{n}^{\prime})}\right\rvert=\min_{y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert
. With this choice we obtain for all
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{x_{n}^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert&\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma_{n}}(x_{n}^{\prime})-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\left(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(x_{n}^{\prime})}\right\rvert\right)\\
&\leq\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert.\end{split}

This implies for any
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}

\omega L(x_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime}))=\omega(\left\lvert{x_{\Sigma}-x_{\Sigma_{n}}(y^{\prime})}\right\rvert+\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)\geq\frac{\omega}{2}\left\lvert{x_{n}^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert.

Moreover,
a\mapsto(a+a^{j})e^{-a}
,
a>0
, is a monotonically decreasing function and therefore we get with
\rho(x_{n}^{\prime},y^{\prime}):=\frac{\omega}{2}\left\lvert{x_{n}^{\prime}-y^{\prime}}\right\rvert

\begin{split}\int_{\Sigma}&(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{j})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\max_{n\in\{1,\dots,p\}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}}\big((\rho(x_{n}^{\prime},y^{\prime})+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)+(\rho(x_{n}^{\prime},y^{\prime})+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)^{j}\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot e^{-\rho(x_{n}^{\prime},y^{\prime})-\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\,dy^{\prime}\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}\big((\rho+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)+(\rho+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)^{j}\big)e^{-\rho-\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\rho^{\theta-2}d\rho\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{\infty}\big((\rho+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)^{\theta-1}+(\rho+\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)^{j+\theta-2}\big)e^{-\rho-\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\,d\rho\\
&=C\bigg(\int_{\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}^{\infty}\rho^{\theta-1}e^{-\rho}\,d\rho+\int_{\omega\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}^{\infty}\rho^{j+\theta-2}e^{-\rho}\,d\rho\bigg)\\
&\leq C\begin{cases}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert,&j=1-\theta,\\
\frac{1}{\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert},&j=-\theta,\end{cases}\end{split}

where
C>0
does not depend on
x_{\Sigma}
,
t
,
s
, and
\varepsilon
. This proves (
B.11
).

Step 2.

In this step we verify the estimate

\|b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})},\quad\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(B.12)

In fact, with the help of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, Lemma
B.1
(i), and (
B.11
) we obtain
for
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma

\begin{split}\big|&b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma})\big|^{2}=\left\lvert{\int_{\Sigma}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\psi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\leq\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 50.0pt\cdot\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

(B.13)

Now, Fubini’s theorem and (
B.11
) show

\begin{split}&\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 20.0pt\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 30.0pt\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 20.0pt=C\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert\\
&\hskip 30.0pt\cdot\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma})\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 20.0pt\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{2}\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}),\end{split}

which yields (
B.12
).

Step 3.
Next we prove the estimate

\|b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\leq C\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})},\quad\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(B.14)

Let
\psi\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})=x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma
with
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{\theta-1}
. By Lemma
B.2
the function
\varphi_{l}
and its derivatives are bounded. Thus, with
\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}:=\varphi_{l}\circ x_{\Sigma_{l}}
we have

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}(x^{\prime})(b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi)(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))\Big)}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\qquad\leq 2\left\lvert{\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}(x^{\prime})\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi)(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}+2\left\lvert{\left(\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}(x^{\prime})\right)b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\qquad\leq C\left(\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi)(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}\right).\end{split}

Using the dominated convergence theorem and the properties of
\Delta G_{z}
stated in Lemma
B.1
one obtains

\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi)(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))=\int_{\Sigma}\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})\psi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).

Hence, we get with the Cauchy Schwarz inequality, Lemma
B.1
(ii),
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})
, and (
B.11
)

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}(b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi)(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}=\left\lvert{\int_{\Sigma}\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})\psi(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\int_{\Sigma}\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{-\theta})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 85.0pt\cdot\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\\
&\hskip 15.0pt\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

According to (
B.13
) we can estimate

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\quad\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{1-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}),\end{split}

where
x_{\Sigma}=x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime})
.
Moreover,
a+a^{1-\theta}\leq 2(a+a^{-\theta})
for
a>0
and
\left\lvert{\log(b)}\right\rvert\leq C\tfrac{1}{b}
for
b\in(0,2\varepsilon_{2})
yields

\begin{split}&\left\lvert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))}\right\rvert^{2}\\
&\hskip 50.0pt\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Thus,

\begin{split}&\int_{x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Big(\varphi_{l}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi\Big)(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\int_{x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})\\
&\hskip 150.0pt\cdot e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,dx^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\int_{x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})\\
&\hskip 120.0pt\cdot e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}),y_{\Sigma})}\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\sqrt{1+\left\lvert{\nabla\zeta_{l}(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}}\,dx^{\prime}\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\frac{\varepsilon}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert}\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})^{{-\theta}})e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})}\,\left\lvert{\psi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})d\sigma(x_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

Therefore, Fubini and (
B.11
) yield

\int_{x_{\Sigma_{l}}^{-1}(\Sigma)}\left\lvert{\frac{d}{dx_{k}^{\prime}}\Big(\varphi_{l}(x_{\Sigma_{l}}(x^{\prime}))b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\psi\Big)(x^{\prime})}\right\rvert^{2}\,dx^{\prime}\leq C\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{2}}{\left\lVert{\psi}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}.

This estimate, the definition of the norm in
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, see
Section
2.1
, and (
B.12
) imply (
B.14
).

Step 4.

By (
2.2
), we have
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})=[L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{1/2}
and using that also
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})=[L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})]_{1/2}

we conclude from the bounds
(
B.12
) and (
B.14
) together with
(
1.18
) that

\begin{split}\|b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)&\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\leq C{\left\lVert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{1/2}{\left\lVert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\to H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{1/2}\\
&\leq C\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}.\end{split}

This completes the proof of Proposition
B.3
.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{A})
. Then, the operator-valued function

\begin{split}&F:(-1,1)^{2}\to\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}),H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\\
&F(t,s)=\begin{cases}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z),&\textup{if }t\neq s,\\
0,&\textup{if }t=s,\end{cases}\end{split}

is measurable.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
{\left({F(\cdot,\cdot)\varphi},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
is measurable on
(-1,1)^{2}
for all
\varphi,\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
; cf. Definition
2.5
. For this, we prove that the function
{\left({F(\cdot,\cdot)\varphi},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
is continuous on
\mathcal{O}:=(-1,1)^{2}\setminus\{(t,t):t\in(-1,1)\}
. Let
(t,s)\in\mathcal{O}
be fixed and consider the case
t>s
.
Choose a sequence
((t_{n},s_{n}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{O}
which converges to
(t,s)
. It is no restriction to assume that
\tfrac{3}{2}(t_{n}-s_{n})>t-s>\frac{1}{2}(t_{n}-s_{n})
holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then,

\begin{split}L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})&=|x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}|+\varepsilon|t_{n}-s_{n}|<|x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}|+2\varepsilon|t-s|\leq 2L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)\end{split}

and in a similar way

L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})^{1-\theta}\leq\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{1-\theta}L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)^{1-\theta}.

Moreover, as
|t_{n}-s_{n}|\geq 0=|t-s|-|t-s|\geq|t-s|-2
, one has

e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})}\leq e^{-\omega(|x_{\Sigma}-y_{\Sigma}|+\varepsilon|t-s|)+2\omega\varepsilon}\leq e^{2\omega\varepsilon_{A}}e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)}.

Combining Lemma
B.1
(i) with the latter three displayed formulas yields the existence of a constant
C>0
which is independent of
x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s,t_{n},s_{n}
, and
\varepsilon
such that

\begin{split}\big|\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})\big|\!&\leq\!C\varepsilon\bigl(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})\!+\!L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})^{{1-\theta}}\bigr)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})}\\
&\leq C\varepsilon\bigl(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)^{{1-\theta}}\bigr)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)}.\end{split}

(B.15)

We claim that
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Let
\gamma\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
be fixed, then

\begin{split}&{\left({(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)-b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z))\varphi},{\gamma}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&\hskip 10.0pt=\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}{\left\langle{(\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t_{n},s_{n})-\Delta G_{z}(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s))\varphi(y_{\Sigma})},{\gamma(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rangle}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma}).\end{split}

The integrand on the right hand side converges pointwise almost everywhere to zero, as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, Lemma
B.1
(i) and (
B.15
) show that the integrand is bounded by
M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\left\lvert{\varphi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\gamma(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert
with

\begin{split}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma}):=C\varepsilon\big(L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)+L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)^{{1-\theta}}\big)e^{-\omega L(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma},t,s)}.\end{split}

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality twice, Fubini’s theorem, and the symmetry relation
M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})=M(y_{\Sigma},x_{\Sigma})
yields

\begin{split}\bigg(\int_{\Sigma}&\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\left\lvert{\varphi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\left\lvert{\gamma(x_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma})\bigg)^{2}\\
\leq&\int_{\Sigma}\bigg(\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\left\lvert{\varphi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\bigg)^{2}\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma}){\left\lVert{\gamma}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\\
\leq&\int_{\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\left\lvert{\varphi(y_{\Sigma})}\right\rvert^{2}\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(x_{\Sigma}){\left\lVert{\gamma}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\\
\leq&C\bigg(\sup_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\bigg)^{2}{\left\lVert{\varphi}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}{\left\lVert{\gamma}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}.\end{split}

Furthermore, with (
B.11
) we see that

\sup_{x_{\Sigma}\in\Sigma}\int_{\Sigma}M(x_{\Sigma},y_{\Sigma})\,d\sigma(y_{\Sigma})\leq C\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert<\infty.

Hence, dominated convergence yields
{\left({(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)-b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z))\varphi},{\gamma}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\to 0
for
n\to\infty
. Since,
\gamma\in L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
was arbitrary, we conclude that
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.

Next, we show that
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
in the space
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, which shows the claimed continuity. For this, we note that Lemma
B.3
and
\tfrac{3}{2}(t_{n}-s_{n})>t-s>0
imply that
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a bounded sequence in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Let us assume that
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does not converge weakly to
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Then, the
H^{1/2}
-boundedness implies that there exists a weakly convergent subsequence
(b_{t_{n_{k}},s_{n_{k}},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges to some
\varphi^{\prime}\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
\varphi^{\prime}\neq b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
. However, in this case
(b_{t_{n_{k}},s_{n_{k}},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
would also converge weakly to
\varphi^{\prime}
in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
which contradicts the first part of the proof. Hence,
(b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to
b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi
in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and therefore,
({\left({b_{t_{n},s_{n},\varepsilon}(z)\varphi},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
{\left({b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)\varphi},{\psi}\right)}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
.
∎

After all these preliminary considerations we are prepared to prove (
3.25
).

Proof of (
3.25
).

Let
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
be fixed. Using Proposition
B.3
, the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and Fubini’s theorem we obtain

\begin{split}&\int_{-1}^{1}\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)f(s)}\right\rVert}_{H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds\bigg)^{2}\,dt\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}\left(\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\,ds\right)^{2}\,dt\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\int_{-1}^{1}\Bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,ds\\
&\hskip 60.0pt\cdot\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,ds\Bigg)dt\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\sup_{s\in(-1,1)}\bigg(\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,dt\bigg)^{2}\int_{-1}^{1}{\left\lVert{f(s)}\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}^{2}\,ds\\
&\hskip 10.0pt\leq C\varepsilon\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert{\left\lVert{f}\right\rVert}_{0}^{2},\end{split}

where we used in the last estimate that for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small one has

\begin{split}\sup_{s\in(-1,1)}\int_{-1}^{1}&\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,dt\\
&=\sup_{t\in(-1,1)}\int_{-1}^{1}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{t-s}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,ds\\
&\leq\int_{-2}^{2}\left\lvert{\varepsilon\log(\varepsilon\left\lvert{\tau}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{\tau}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,d\tau\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert^{1/2}\int_{-2}^{2}(1+\left\lvert{\log(\left\lvert{\tau}\right\rvert)}\right\rvert)^{1/2}\frac{1}{\left\lvert{\tau}\right\rvert^{1/2}}\,d\tau\\
&\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert^{1/2}.\end{split}

Combined with Lemma
B.4
and the discussion below Definition
2.5
this shows that

\int_{-1}^{1}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)f(s)\,ds\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
exists for a.e.
t\in(-1,1)
and that the function
t\mapsto\int_{-1}^{1}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)f(s)\,ds\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
is measurable. Hence, the mapping

\begin{split}\mathcal{B}&:L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))\to L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})),\\
\mathcal{B}f(t)&:=\int_{-1}^{1}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)f(s)\,ds,\end{split}

is well-defined, bounded, and
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{0\to 1/2}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}\left\lvert{\log(\varepsilon)}\right\rvert^{1/2}
. By (
B.2
), (
B.3
), and Proposition
2.7
(iii) we also have

(\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z))f(t)=\int_{-1}^{1}b_{t,s,\varepsilon}(z)f(s)\,ds=\mathcal{B}f(t)

for all
f\in L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
. Hence,
\widetilde{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon}(z)
can be extended to a bounded operator from
L^{2}((-1,1);L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
to
L^{2}((-1,1);H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}))
and (
3.25
) is true, i.e. all claims are shown.
∎

Appendix C
Properties of
\Phi_{z}
and
\mathcal{C}_{z}

This appendix is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.8
and Proposition
2.9
. For that, we follow a similar strategy as in
[
10
]
, where similar results for bounded
\Sigma
were shown. We also refer to
[
7
,
8
,
9
,
13
,
34
]
, where related results were shown.

Throughout this appendix, let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and let
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{\theta}
satisfy Hypothesis
2.1
. We define in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
the operator
T
by

\begin{split}Tu&:=\big(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta\big)u_{+}\oplus\big(-i(\alpha\cdot\nabla)+m\beta\big)u_{-},\\
\textup{dom }T&:=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}):=H^{1}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{1}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N}),\end{split}

and the mappings
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{1}:\textup{dom }T\to H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
by

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}u

\displaystyle:=i(\alpha\cdot\nu)\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}u

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}u_{+}+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}u_{-}\right).

Recall that
\nu
is pointing outwards of
\Omega_{+}
. For
u_{\pm},v_{\pm}\in H^{1}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})

integration by parts implies

\big((\alpha\cdot\nabla)u_{\pm},v_{\pm}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})}=-\big(u_{\pm},(\alpha\cdot\nabla)v_{\pm}\big)_{L^{2}(\Omega_{\pm};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\pm\big((\alpha\cdot\nu)u_{\pm},v_{\pm}\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}

and one finds in the same way as, e.g., in the proof of
[
10
, Theorem 4.3 (i)]
that

{\left({Tu},{v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}-{\left({u},{Tv}\right)}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}={\left({\Gamma_{1}u},{\Gamma_{0}v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}-{\left({\Gamma_{0}u},{\Gamma_{1}v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}

(C.1)

holds for all
u,v\in\textup{dom }T
.
Note that (
C.1
) implies that
T\upharpoonright\ker\Gamma_{0}
is symmetric. Furthermore,
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})\subset\ker\Gamma_{0}
. Since the free Dirac operator
H
in (
2.8
) is self-adjoint, this implies that

T\upharpoonright\ker\Gamma_{0}=H.

(C.2)

Now, we are prepared to prove Proposition
2.8
and Proposition
2.9
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

First, Fubini’s theorem shows the representation in (
2.13
). Hence, the mapping properties of
\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}
and
R_{z}
prove assertion (iii).

To verify item (i), we note first that by (iii) and anti-duality
\Phi_{z}
has the bounded extension

\widetilde{\Phi}_{z}:=(\Phi_{z}^{*})^{\prime}:H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})=H^{0}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{0}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N}).

(C.3)

Next, we show the statement for
r=\frac{1}{2}
. If we manage to do that, then the claim for
r\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
follows from (
C.3
) and interpolation.

To prove the claim for
r=\frac{1}{2}
we note that with (
C.2
) one can show for
z\in\rho(H)
the direct sum decomposition

\textup{dom }T=\textup{dom }H\dot{+}\ker(T-z)=\ker\Gamma_{0}\dot{+}\ker(T-z),

which allows us to define the auxiliary operator

\widehat{\Phi}_{z}:=(\Gamma_{0}\upharpoonright\ker(T-z))^{-1}.

(C.4)

Note that the properties of the trace operator in Proposition
2.2
imply that
\textup{ran }\Gamma_{0}=H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and we also have
\textup{dom }T=H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Thus,
\widehat{\Phi}_{z}
is a linear operator from
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Next, we show that
\widehat{\Phi}_{z}
is a restriction of
\Phi_{z}
. To see this, we observe for
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
,
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, and
u=R_{\overline{z}}v=(H-\overline{z})^{-1}v\in\textup{dom }H=\ker\Gamma_{0}
with the help of (
C.1
) that

\begin{split}\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,v\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}&=\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,(H-\overline{z})u\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,Hu\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\big(z\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,u\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,Tu\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\big(T\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,u\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&=-\big(\Gamma_{1}\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,\Gamma_{0}u\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}+\big(\Gamma_{0}\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,\Gamma_{1}u\big)_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left({\varphi},{\Gamma_{1}R_{\overline{z}}v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}={\left({\varphi},{(\Phi_{z})^{*}v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left({\Phi_{z}\varphi},{v}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}.\end{split}

Since this holds for all
v\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
we conclude that
\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi=\Phi_{z}\varphi
, i.e.
\widehat{\Phi}_{z}=\Phi_{z}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. In particular,
\Phi_{z}\varphi\in\ker(T-z)
by (
C.4
), which yields item (ii).
Eventually, we show that this restriction of
\Phi_{z}
is bounded from
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. To see this, we show that
\widehat{\Phi}_{z}
is closed with respect to these spaces.
But this follows from the
L^{2}
boundedness of
\Phi_{z}
and the fact that
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta}\setminus\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
are continuously embedded in
L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})
, respectively. Thus, the closed graph theorem shows that

\widehat{\Phi}_{z}=\Phi_{z}\upharpoonright H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N}):H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\rightarrow H^{1}(\Omega_{+};\mathbb{C}^{N})\oplus H^{1}(\Omega_{-};\mathbb{C}^{N})

is bounded, which finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.9
.

(i) First, it follows from Proposition
2.8
(i) and (
2.14
) that
\mathcal{C}_{z}
is a bounded operator in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. Next, we show that the anti-dual
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}
, which is a bounded map in
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, is an extension of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
. To see this, let
\varphi,\psi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
. We use (
C.1
), Proposition
2.8
(ii), (
C.4
), and the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
to obtain

\begin{split}0&=\big(T\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,\widehat{\Phi}_{\overline{z}}\psi\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}-\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi,T\widehat{\Phi}_{\overline{z}}\psi\big)_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{\theta};\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left({\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi},{\psi}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}-{\left({\varphi},{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}\psi}\right)}_{L^{2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left\langle{\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi},{\psi}\right\rangle}_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}-{\left\langle{\varphi},{\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}\psi}\right\rangle}_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}\\
&={\left\langle{(\mathcal{C}_{z}-\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}^{\prime})\varphi},{\psi}\right\rangle}_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})},\end{split}

where
{\left\langle{\cdot},{\cdot}\right\rangle}_{H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})}
denotes the sesquilinear duality product, which is anti-linear in the second argument, on
H^{-1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})\times H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
Hence,
\mathcal{C}_{\overline{z}}^{\prime}
is an extension of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
which is bounded in
H^{-{1/2}}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
, i.e. the claim is true for
r=-\frac{1}{2}
. By interpolation, we conclude that
\mathcal{C}_{z}
gives rise to a bounded map in
H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
for any
r\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
.

(ii) First, for
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
the definition of
\mathcal{C}_{z}
in (
2.14
) and the relation (
C.4
) imply

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}_{z}\varphi&=\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{+}+\frac{1}{2}\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{-}\\
&=\mp\frac{1}{2}\left(\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{+}\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi\big)_{+}-\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{-}\big(\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi\big)_{-}\right)+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{\pm}\\
&=\pm\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\Gamma_{0}\widehat{\Phi}_{z}\varphi+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{\pm}\\
&=\pm\frac{i}{2}(\alpha\cdot\nu)\varphi+\boldsymbol{t}_{\Sigma}^{\pm}\left(\Phi_{z}\varphi\right)_{\pm},\end{split}

(C.5)

which is the claimed identity for
\varphi\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
.
If
\varphi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
and
r\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, then the assertion follows from (
C.5
) by continuity and density.
∎

Remark C.1
.

We note that the results stated in Proposition
2.8
and Proposition
2.9
are not optimal
in the sense of the maximal possible range of Sobolev indices
r
; the stated mapping properties can be proved for a wider range of Sobolev indices in a similar manner as in
[
10
, Proposition 4.4 and Corollary 4.5]
, but the present formulation is sufficient for the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
More precisely, Proposition
2.8
(i) can be extended to
r\in[-\tfrac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
and (ii) also remains valid for

\varphi\in H^{r}(\Sigma;\mathbb{C}^{N})
with
r\in[-\tfrac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
. Furthermore, the Plemelj-Sokhotsky formula in Proposition
2.9
(ii) can be generalized to all
r\in[-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
.
